You don't have to make just one kind of bet for each spin, you can make as many as you like, and you win if the ball lands on any of your numbers.  If you're playing at a lively table, players will aggressively throw chips all over the layout—5, 10, 26, street bets, avenue bets bets, and odd and black for good measure—all for the same spin.  People even bet on individual numbers even when those numbers were already covered by another bet (such as a street bet).  If a player bet on #11 and also made a street bet on 10-12, and #11 came up, he'd win both bets.  If #12 came up, he'd win the street bet but lose the bet on #11.  So players who throw a lot of chips around the table usually have a lot of chips coming back to them at the end of each spin in winnings.  This doesn't change the house edge; it's the same whether you make one bet per spin or several.  But you will likely lose money faster the more bets you make, because you're betting more.
Assuming that the possible bets are all understood, Roulette is essentially a trivially simple game to play. For each turn, once all bets have been placed using coloured chips to distinguish each player, the croupier halts betting, spins the wheel, and rolls the ball in the opposite direction. When the ball comes to a halt in one of the slots, the croupier announces the result, collects all losing bets and pays out the winner's profits.

Please Note: The Martingale is much like the iceberg that sunk the Titanic. The loss of six to eight hands in a row seems like a real longshot; but the fact is that anyone who has played roulette has seen streaks of red or black, odd or even, or high or low coming up with such frequency many times. Casinos put a cap on how much a person can bet so that such relatively short streaks can sink the Martingale player.
The D’Alembert system was invented by the 18th century French mathematician Jean-Baptiste le Rond d’Alembert and is perhaps the easiest of all Roulette strategies to apply. As a negative progression system, it involves you placing a bet, adding one unit to it if you lose, or removing a unit from it if you win, i.e. raise when you lose, lower when you win. Predicated on the idea of natural equilibrium, the D’Alembert strategy works best when applied to a set of even wins and losses for the same bet – but of course you won’t know what the Roulette wheel has in store for you until you start to play.
Most players don’t understand is this is no different to 4 different players making 4 different bets. And the odds of winning and payout are the same regardless. So what has the player changed with progression? Absolutely nothing except the amount they bet. The chances of winning or losing are the same on each spin. So if your system doesn’t win with flat betting (no progression), then it will fail with progression.
Casinos will normally post a maximum and a minimum stake for a roulette table and this is sometimes done for recreational play, too. Typically, for each spin of the wheel, if a player the total amount of a player's inside bets must exceed the minimum stake. The listed maximum stake usually shows only the maximum allowed for a single number "straight up" bet. The maximum stakes for other types of bet increases proportionately e.g. The maximum bet allowed for a pair of numbers is double the maximum straight-up bet, the maximum allowed for a corner bet is 4 times the straight up maximum and so on. So that really the limitation is on the amount that the casino can lose!

It goes without saying that those who play American roulette that features two zeroes will have to deal with a bigger house edge. By contrast, those who prefer French Roulette, which is a version of European Roulette will have better chances to win, due to the “in prison” rule. Basically, if the ball lands on zero, the player doesn’t lose the wager, instead it is locked in for another spin. If the next wager is a winner, the money will be released, if not it goes to the casino’s coffers.
Roulette games have minimum bets, which will be posted on a placard at the table.  Minimum bets work differently for inside bets (specific numbers) and outside bets (everything else).  For outside bets, any bet you make has to be at least the table minimum.  Inside bets can usually be as small as you like, as long as the total of all your inside bets is the table minimum.  For example, with a table minimum of \$5, you could put \$5 on #14, or \$1 each on #14, #27, #8<#sep#>You are given a dataset of sales records for a company's products. Each record contains the following fields: `product_id`, `quantity`, `price`, and `date`. Your task is to implement a function `calculate_sales_summary(sales)` that processes this dataset and returns a dictionary. The dictionary should contain the total sales amount and the average quantity sold for each product. The total sales amount is computed by multiplying the quantity and price for each sale. The average quantity sold is determined by dividing the total quantity sold by the number of sales for each product.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a sale record with keys `product_id`, `quantity`, `price`, and `date`.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where each key is a `product_id` and the value is another dictionary containing:
  - `total_sales`: The total sales amount for the product.
  - `average_quantity`: The average quantity sold for the product.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 1, 'price': 9.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 3, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 19.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'p1': {'total_sales': 71.94, 'average_quantity': 2.5},
    'p2': {'total_sales': 49.97, 'average_quantity': 1.5}
}
```

```python
def calculate_sales_summary(sales):
    """
    Processes a list of sales records and returns a summary dictionary containing
    the total sales amount and the average quantity sold for each product.

    :param sales: List of dictionaries, each containing 'product_id', 'quantity', 'price', and 'date'.
    :return: Dictionary with product_id as keys and a nested dictionary with 'total_sales' and 'average_quantity' as values.
    """
    summary = {}
    
    for sale in sales:
        product_id = sale['product_id']
        quantity = sale['quantity']
        price = sale['price']
        total_sales = quantity * price
        
        if product_id not in summary:
            summary[product_id] = {'total_sales': 0, 'total_quantity': 0, 'count': 0}
        
        summary[product_id]['total_sales'] += total_sales
        summary[product_id]['total_quantity'] += quantity
        summary[product_id]['count'] += 1

    for product_id in summary:
        summary[product_id]['average_quantity'] = summary[product_id]['total_quantity'] / summary[product_id]['count']
        del summary[product_id]['total_quantity']
        del summary[product_id]['count']

    return summary

# Test the function with the provided sales data
sales = [
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 1, 'price': 9.99, 'date': '2023-01-01'},
    {'product_id': 'p1', 'quantity': 3, 'price': 15.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'},
    {'product_id': 'p2', 'quantity': 2, 'price': 19.99, 'date': '2023-01-02'}
]

print(calculate_sales_summary(sales))
```<#sep#> another.

To reproduce: https://github.com/spulec/moto/pull/4818

The behavior between `sns.publish` and `sns.publish_batch` seems to be slightly different, in `sns.publish` the message attributes are handled with `SNSResponse._parse_message_attributes`.

This leads to an exception being raised in `SNSModel.publish_batch` that's not a `InvalidParameterValue` so the message is not listed in neither `"Failed"` or `"Succeeded"` in the response:
https://github.com/spulec/moto/blob/0a1bb6bae165b38cdec5e299a7f859f2cb27de93/moto/sns/models.py#L887-L888

In `SNSResponse.publish_batch` I tried hardcoding the `"MessageAttributes"` to the same format that's provided by `_parse_message_attributes` which seemed to make it work (besides the attribute being hardcoded of course)

Roulette games have minimum bets, which will be posted on a placard at the table.  Minimum bets work differently for inside bets (specific numbers) and outside bets (everything else).  For outside bets, any bet you make has to be at least the table minimum.  Inside bets can usually be as small as you like, as long as the total of all your inside bets is the table minimum.  For example, with a table minimum of \$5, you could put \$5 on #14, or \$1 each on #14, #27, #8/9, #28/29/30, and #19/20/22/23.  Remember that you can bet inside or outside if you like; there's no requirement to bet both on a given spin.

The class should have the following functionalities:

- **Attributes**: 
  - `name`: A string representing the employee's name.
  - `employee_id`: An integer representing the employee's unique identifier.
  - `department`: A string representing the department where the employee works.
  - `salary`: A float representing the employee's annual salary.

- **Methods**:
  - `display_info`: A method that prints the employee's details in the following format:
    ```
    Employee Name: [name]
    Employee ID: [employee_id]
    Department: [department]
    Salary: $[salary] per year
    ```
  - `set_salary(new_salary)`: A method that updates the employee's salary only if the new salary is greater than the current salary. If the new salary is not greater, it should print:
    ```
    The new salary must be greater than the current salary.
    ```
  - `company_policy`: A static method that prints the company's employee policy:
    ```
    Company Employee Policy:
    Minimum salary is $30,000 per year.
    The company does not tolerate any form of discrimination.
    ```

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input:
  ```python
  emp = Employee("John Doe", 12345, "Engineering", 50000)
  emp.display_info()
  emp.set_salary(55000)
  emp.display_info()
  Employee.company_policy()
  ```
- Output:
  ```
  Employee Name: John Doe
  Employee ID: 12345
  Department: Engineering
  Salary: $50,000.00 per year
  Employee Name: John Doe
  Employee ID: 12345
  Department: Engineering
  Salary: $55,000.00 per year
  Company Employee Policy:
  Minimum salary is $30,000 per year.
  The company does not tolerate any form of discrimination.
  ```

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, department, salary):
        """
        Initializes a new Employee instance with the given details.

        :param name: str, the name of the employee
        :param employee_id: int, the unique identifier of the employee
        :param department: str, the department where the employee works
        :param salary: float, the annual salary of the employee
        """
        self.__name = name
        self.__employee_id = employee_id
        self.__department = department
        self.__salary = salary

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the employee's details in a formatted string.
        """
        print(f"Employee Name: {self.__name}")
        print(f"Employee ID: {self.__employee_id}")
        print(f"Department: {self.__department}")
        print(f"Salary: ${self.__salary:,.2f} per year")

    def set_salary(self, new_salary):
        """
        Updates the employee's salary if the new salary is greater than the current salary.

        :param new_salary: float, the new salary to be set
        """
        if new_salary > self.__salary:
            self.__salary = new_salary
        else:
            print("The new salary must be greater than the current salary.")

    @staticmethod
    def company_policy():
        """
        Prints the company's employee policy.
        """
        print("Company Employee Policy:")
        print("Minimum salary is $30,000 per year.")
        print("The company does not tolerate any form of discrimination.")

 Subject Name No of Questions Marks General Knowledge & Current Affairs 20 40 Test of Language (Bengali/Nepali) 20 40 English 20 40<#sep#>In a library management system, you are tasked with designing a database schema using Django models. Your task is to create two models: `Genre` and `Book`. The `Genre` model should have a single field `name` which is a unique string. The `Book` model should have the following fields:
- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `published_date`: A date representing when the book was published.
- `isbn`: A unique string representing the ISBN number of the book.
- `genre`: A foreign key linking to the `Genre` model.
- `available_copies`: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.
- `description`: A text field providing a description of the book.

Your task is to write the Django model code for both `Genre` and `Book` models. Ensure that the `__str__` method is properly defined for both models.

**Sample Input:**
```python
Genre.objects.create(name="Science Fiction")
Book.objects.create(
    title="Dune",
    author="Frank Herbert",
    published_date="1965-08-01",
    isbn="9780441172719",
    genre=Genre.objects.get(name="Science Fiction"),
    available_copies=5,
    description="A monumental science fiction novel."
)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Dune
Science Fiction
```

```python
from django.db import models

class Genre(models.Model):
    """
    Model representing a book genre.
    
    Fields:
    - name: A unique string representing the name of the genre.
    """
    name = models.CharField(max_length=100, unique=True)

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns the name of the genre.
        """
        return self.name

class Book(models.Model):
    """
    Model representing a book in the library.
    
    Fields:
    - title: A string representing the title of the book.
    - author: A string representing the author of the book.
    - published_date: A date representing when the book was published.
    - isbn: A unique string representing the ISBN number of the book.
    - genre: A foreign key linking to the Genre model.
    - available_copies: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.
    - description: A text field providing a description of the book.
    """
    title = models.CharField(max_length=200)
    author = models.CharField(max_length=100)
    published_date = models.DateField()
    isbn = models.CharField(max_length=13, unique=True)
    genre = models.ForeignKey(Genre, on_delete=models.CASCADE)
    available_copies = models.IntegerField(default=0)
    description = models.TextField()

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns the title of the book.
        """
        return self.title
```<#sep#>First, expand the left side:
\[
(xy - 1)^2 = x^2y^2 - 2xy + 1
\]

Next, expand the right side:
\[
(x + 1)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = x^2 + 2x + 1 + y^2 + 2y + 1 = x^2 + y^2 + 2x + 2y + 2
\]

Set the two expanded sides equal:
\[
x^2y^2 - 2xy + 1 = x^2 + y^2 + 2x + 2y + 2
\]

Rearrange all terms to one side:
\[
x^2y^2 - 2xy + 1 - x^2 - y^2 - 2x - 2y - 2 = 0
\]

Simplify the constants:
\[
x^2y^2 - x^2 - y^2 - 2xy - 2x - 2y - 1 = 0
\]

Now, we will test specific values to find potential solutions.

1. **Case \(x = -1\):**
   \[
   (-1 \cdot y - 1)^2 = (-y - 1)^2 = (y + 1)^2
   \]
   \[
   (-1 + 1)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 0 + (y + 1)^2 = (y + 1)^2
   \]
   Both sides are equal, so \((x, y) = (-1, y)\) for any integer \(y\) is a solution.

2. **Case \(y = -1\):**
   \[
   (x \cdot (-1) - 1)^2 = (-x - 1)^2 = (x + 1)^2
   \]
   \[
   (x + 1)^2 + (-1 + 1)^2 = (x + 1)^2 + 0 = (x + 1)^2
   \]
   Both sides are equal, so \((x, y) = (x, -1)\) for any integer \(x\) is a solution.

3. **Testing specific values for \(x\) and \(y\):**

   - For \(x = 0\):
     \[
     (0 \cdot y - 1)^2 = 1
     \]
     \[
     (0 + 1)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 1 + (y + 1)^2
     \]
     Set equal:
     \[
     1 = 1 + (y + 1)^2 \implies (y + 1)^2 = 0 \implies y = -1
     \]
     So, \((0, -1)\) is a solution.

   - For \(y = 0\):
     \[
     (x \cdot 0 - 1)^2 = 1
     \]
     \[
     (x + 1)^2 + (0 + 1)^2 = (x + 1)^2 + 1
     \]
     Set equal:
     \[
     1 = (x + 1)^2 + 1 \implies (x + 1)^2 = 0 \implies x = -1
     \]
     So, \((-1, 0)\) is a solution.

   - For \(x = 1\):
     \[
     (1 \cdot y - 1)^2 = (y - 1)^2
     \]
     \[
     (1 + 1)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 + (y + 1)^2
     \]
     Set equal:
     \[
     (y - 1)^2 = 4 + (y + 1)^2 \implies y^2 - 2y + 1 = 4 + y^2 + 2y + 1 \implies -4y = 4 \implies y = -1
     \]
     So, \((1, -1)\) is a solution.

   - For \(y = 1\):
     \[
     (x \cdot 1 - 1)^2 = (x - 1)^2
     \]
     \[
     (x + 1)^2 + (1 + 1)^2 = (x + 1)^2 + 4
     \]
     Set equal:
     \[
     (x - 1)^2 = (x + 1)^2 + 4 \implies x^2 - 2x + 1 = x^2 + 2x + 1 + 4 \implies -4x = 4 \implies x = -1
     \]
     So, \((-1, 1)\) is a solution.

   - For \(x = 2\):
     \[
     (2 \cdot y - 1)^2 = (2y - 1)^2
     \]
     \[
     (2 + 1)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 9 + (y + 1)^2
     \]
     Set equal:
     \[
     (2y - 1)^2 = 9 + (y + 1)^2 \implies 4y^2 - 4y + 1 = 9 + y^2 + 2y + 1 \implies 3y^2 - 6y - 9 = 0 \implies y^2 - 2y - 3 = 0 \implies (y - 3)(y + 1) = 0 \implies y = 3 \text{ or } y = -1
     \]
     So, \((2, 3)\) and \((2, -1)\) are solutions.

   - For \(x = 3\):
     \[
     (3 \cdot y - 1)^2 = (3y - 1)^2
     \]
     \[
     (3 + 1)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 + (y + 1)^2
     \]
     Set equal:
     \[
     (3y - 1)^2 = 16 + (y + 1)^2 \implies 9y^2 - 6y + 1 = 16 + y^2 + 2y + 1 \implies 8y^2 - 8y - 16 = 0 \implies y^2 - y - 2 = 0 \implies (y - 2)(y + 1) = 0 \implies y = 2 \text{ or } y = -1
     \]
     So, \((3, 2)\) and \((3, -1)\) are solutions.

Thus, the complete set of solutions is:
\[
\boxed{ \{ (x, -1) \mid x \in \mathbb{Z} \} \cup \{ (-1, y) \mid y \in \mathbb{Z} \} \cup \{ (2, 3), (3, 2) \} }
\]<#sep#>4 (p-1 is 4).

to make sure it's positive. Because in some cases (k-d_k) mod p<#sep#>CORS rules not being applied to PUT responses
Hello! I'm the author of [this issue](https://github.com/getmoto/moto/issues/4220) which was solved on [this PR](https://github.com/getmoto/moto/pull/4497). So first off I'd like to thank you for looking into it.

Some time has passed but only now we've been able to update to the new Moto and try to get rid of our workaround. Unfortunately, the change did not solve the issue for us.

Moto version: 4.1.0.dev0 (Moto standalone server)
How I installed it:

sudo apt install python-pip
pip install "moto[s3]"
pip install flask
pip install flask_cors
Environment: pipenv with Python 3.8.0 running on an Ubuntu 18.04.6 LTS machine.

The original problem was that Moto for S3 accepted GetBucketCors requests but didn't apply the CORS rules later on. We in particular needed to set `"ExposeHeaders": ["ETag"]` so that Moto would send an `Access-Control-Expose-Headers: ETag` header in the response. This is because we're using the standalone Moto server and working with a browser controlled by Selenium, and the browser won't let the S3 client running inside it ([EvaporateJS](https://github.com/TTLabs/EvaporateJS)) read the ETag if that header isn't present.

In the current version, if we add a CORS rule, Moto takes it into consideration when responding to a CORS preflight request, which is an OPTIONS request. However, our upload still isn't working. After some more investigation and debugging, we came to the conclusion that it's not enough for the browser to receive the `Access-Control-Expose-Headers: ETag` in the preflight response, the server also needs to provide that header when responding to each individual PUT request that the client does to upload the file parts. 

Here's how Amazon S3 responds to our PUT requests when we upload each part:

![image](https://github.com/getmoto/moto/assets/4132918/fc8ea5ff-b928-4dab-8313-e5e792a815db)

And here's the same response from Moto:

![image](https://github.com/getmoto/moto/assets/4132918/e0f6b3e8-39c3-4e11-a222-b9b0d9908742)

I used BurpSuite to debug the scenario, and if I add the missing header `Access-Control-Expose-Headers: ETag` to the Moto response for each part's PUT, it works fine. The header is present in the preflight response thanks to the fix from the PR I mentioned, it's just missing from the PUT response.

I looked a little bit into the code and it looks like the extra headers are added in the `_set_cors_headers()` in moto/s3/responses.py. This method is only called from `_response_options()` which handles OPTIONS request. I'm guessing something similar needs to be done in `_key_response_put()` but I don't have the expertise in this project to do it myself.

Could you please look into this one more time? Thank you very much in advance!!


diff --git a/moto/s3/responses.py b/moto/s3/responses.py
--- a/moto/s3/responses.py
+++ b/moto/s3/responses.py
@@ -353,7 +353,9 @@ def _bucket_response_head(
             return 404, {}, ""
         return 200, {"x-amz-bucket-region": bucket.region_name}, ""
 
-    def _set_cors_headers(self, headers: Dict[str, str], bucket: FakeBucket) -> None:
+    def _set_cors_headers_options(
+        self, headers: Dict[str, str], bucket: FakeBucket
+    ) -> None:
         """
         TODO: smarter way of matching the right CORS rule:
         See https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/cors.html
@@ -408,10 +410,57 @@ def _response_options(
             # AWS S3 seems to return 403 on OPTIONS and 404 on GET/HEAD
             return 403, {}, ""
 
-        self._set_cors_headers(headers, bucket)
+        self._set_cors_headers_options(headers, bucket)
 
         return 200, self.response_headers, ""
 
+    def _get_cors_headers_other(
+        self, headers: Dict[str, str], bucket_name: str
+    ) -> Dict[str, Any]:
+        """
+        Returns a dictionary with the appropriate CORS headers
+        Should be used for non-OPTIONS requests only
+        Applicable if the 'Origin' header matches one of a CORS-rules - returns an empty dictionary otherwise
+        """
+        response_headers: Dict[str, Any] = dict()
+        try:
+            origin = headers.get("Origin")
+            if not origin:
+                return response_headers
+            bucket = self.backend.get_bucket(bucket_name)
+
+            def _to_string(header: Union[List[str], str]) -> str:
+                # We allow list and strs in header values. Transform lists in comma-separated strings
+                if isinstance(header, list):
+                    return ", ".join(header)
+                return header
+
+            for cors_rule in bucket.cors:
+                if cors_rule.allowed_origins is not None:
+                    if cors_matches_origin(origin, cors_rule.allowed_origins):  # type: ignore
+                        response_headers["Access-Control-Allow-Origin"] = origin  # type: ignore
+                        if cors_rule.allowed_methods is not None:
+                            response_headers[
+                                "Access-Control-Allow-Methods"
+                            ] = _to_string(cors_rule.allowed_methods)
+                        if cors_rule.allowed_headers is not None:
+                            response_headers[
+                                "Access-Control-Allow-Headers"
+                            ] = _to_string(cors_rule.allowed_headers)
+                        if cors_rule.exposed_headers is not None:
+                            response_headers[
+                                "Access-Control-Expose-Headers"
+                            ] = _to_string(cors_rule.exposed_headers)
+                        if cors_rule.max_age_seconds is not None:
+                            response_headers["Access-Control-Max-Age"] = _to_string(
+                                cors_rule.max_age_seconds
+                            )
+
+                        return response_headers
+        except S3ClientError:
+            pass
+        return response_headers

 WBPSC Results WBPSC Recruitment WBPSC Admit Card West Bengal Judicial Service Examination How To Write On Exam Paper Faster & Better How To Reduce Negative Marking In Exam 11 Tips To Remember What You Read How to Score Good Marks in Exam<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a RESTful API using Flask that supports CRUD operations on a collection of books. The API must include the following endpoints:

- `GET /books`: Return a JSON object containing a list of all books.
- `GET /books/<id>`: Return a JSON object representing the book with the specified ID. If the book does not exist, return a 404 error with a message indicating the book was not found.
- `POST /books`: Create a new book. The request body must be a JSON object containing at least the fields `title` and `author`. If the request body is invalid, return a 400 error with a message indicating the required fields.
- `PUT /books/<id>`: Update the book with the specified ID. The request body must be a JSON object. If the book does not exist, return a 404 error. If the request body is invalid, return a 400 error.
- `DELETE /books/<id>`: Delete the book with the specified ID. If the book does not exist, return a 404 error.

The books should be stored in an in-memory list. The API should return appropriate HTTP status codes and error messages.

**Sample Input/Output:**

1. `GET /books`
   - Response: `200 OK`
   - Body: `{"books": [{"id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell"}, {"id": 2, "title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald"}, {"id": 3, "title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee"}]}`

2. `GET /books/2`
   - Response: `200 OK`
   - Body: `{"id": 2, "title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald"}`

3. `POST /books` with body `{"title": "Brave New World", "author": "Aldous Huxley"}`
   - Response: `201 Created`
   - Body: `{"id": 4, "title": "Brave New World", "author": "Aldous Huxley"}`

4. `PUT /books/1` with body `{"title": "Animal Farm"}`
   - Response: `200 OK`
   - Body: `{"id": 1, "title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell"}`

5. `DELETE /books/3`
   - Response: `200 OK`
   - Body: `{"result": true}`

```python
from flask import Flask, jsonify, request, abort

app = Flask(__name__)

# Sample data with a list of dictionaries, acting as an in-memory 'database'
books = [
    {'id': 1, 'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell'},
    {'id': 2, 'title': 'The Great Gatsby', 'author': 'F. Scott Fitzgerald'},
    {'id': 3, 'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee'}
]

def find_book(book_id):
    """
    Helper function to find a book by its ID.
    
    :param book_id: The ID of the book to find.
    :return: The book dictionary if found, otherwise None.
    """
    return next((book for book in books if book['id'] == book_id), None)

@app.route('/books', methods=['GET'])
def get_books():
    """
    Retrieve a list of all books.
    
    :return: JSON response containing the list of books.
    """
    return jsonify({'books': books})

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_book(book_id):
    """
    Retrieve a single book by its ID.
    
    :param book_id: The ID of the book to retrieve.
    :return: JSON response containing the book if found, otherwise 404 error.
    """
    book = find_book(book_id)
    if book is not None:
        return jsonify(book)
    else:
        abort(404, description=f"Book with ID {book_id} not found")

@app.route('/books', methods=['POST'])
def create_book():
    """
    Create a new book.
    
    :return: JSON response containing the new book if successful, otherwise 400 error.
    """
    if not request.json or 'title' not in request.json or 'author' not in request.json:
        abort(400, description="Bad Request: 'title' and 'author' are required fields")
    
    new_id = books[-1]['id'] + 1 if books else 1
    new_book = {
        'id': new_id,
        'title': request.json['title'],
        'author': request.json['author']
    }
    books.append(new_book)
    return jsonify(new_book), 201

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_book(book_id):
    """
    Update an existing book.
    
    :param book_id: The ID of the book to update.
    :return: JSON response containing the updated book if successful, otherwise 400 or 404 error.
    """
    book = find_book(book_id)
    if book is None:
        abort(404, description=f"Book with ID {book_id} not found")

    if not request.json:
        abort(400, description="Bad Request: request body must be JSON")

    book['title'] = request.json.get('title', book['title'])
    book['author'] = request.json.get('author', book['author'])
    return jsonify(book)

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_book(book_id):
    """
    Delete a book.
    
    :param book_id: The ID of the book to delete.
    :return: JSON response indicating success if successful, otherwise 404 error.
    """
    book = find_book(book_id)
    if book is None:
        abort(404, description=f"Book with ID {book_id} not found")

    books.remove(book)
    return jsonify({'result': True})

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)
```<#sep#>The teacher gave each of her 37 students 36 pencils in different colors. It turned out that each pair of students received exactly one pencil of the same color. Determine the smallest possible number of different colors of pencils distributed.

<think>
Okay, let's try to figure out this problem. So, the teacher has 37 students, each gets 36 pencils of different colors. The key point is that each pair of students shares exactly one pencil of the same color. We need to find the smallest number of different colors distributed.

Hmm, let me start by understanding the problem again. Each student has 36 distinct colors, so no two pencils a student has are the same color. Now, for any two students, there's exactly one color that they both have. So, if I pick any two students, they share one common color, but all other colors are different between them. The question is asking for the minimal total number of different colors used across all students.

I think this is a combinatorial problem, maybe related to design theory. Like, something called a projective plane or block design? Let me recall. In a projective plane of order n, each pair of lines intersects in exactly one point, and each line contains n+1 points. The number of points is n² + n + 1. Wait, maybe that's similar here. If we think of each student as a "line" and each color as a "point," then the condition is that any two lines (students) intersect in exactly one point (color). Then the number of points (colors) would be n² + n + 1, and each line has n+1 points. Here, each student has 36 pencils, so n+1 = 36, which would mean n = 35. Then the number of colors would be 35² + 35 + 1 = 35*36 +1 = 1260 +1=1261. But there are 37 students here. Wait, in projective planes, the number of lines is equal to the number of points. So if we have 37 students (lines), we would need 37 points (colors). But according to the formula, for n=35, the number of points is 1261, which is way more than 37. So maybe that's not the right model here.

Alternatively, maybe this is a case of a Balanced Incomplete Block Design (BIBD). In BIBD terms, we have parameters v (number of elements), b (number of blocks), r (number of blocks each element is in), k (size of each block), and λ (each pair of blocks intersects in λ elements). Wait, in our problem, each student is a block (since they have a set of colors), each color is an element. The conditions are that each block (student) has size 36, each pair of blocks (students) intersects in exactly 1 element (color). So, we can model this as a BIBD with parameters v (number of colors), b=37 (number of students), r (number of students each color is given to), k=36 (each student has 36 colors), λ=1 (each pair of students shares one color).

The BIBD parameters must satisfy some equations. The key ones are:

1. b * k = v * r. Because each of the b blocks has k elements, so total elements counted with multiplicity are b*k. Each element is in r blocks, so total is also v*r. Therefore, 37 * 36 = v * r.

2. λ * (v - 1) = r * (k - 1). Wait, another equation: For a BIBD, each pair of elements is contained in exactly λ blocks. Wait, but in our case, the problem states that each pair of blocks (students) share exactly one element (color). That is, the intersection of any two blocks is exactly λ =1. So the standard BIBD parameters are (v, b, r, k, λ), but here the λ refers to the number of blocks that contain any pair of elements, not the intersection of two blocks. So maybe I confused the parameters here.

We need to find the minimal N.

Sum x_i² - 1332 = 1332

So Sum x_i² = 1332 + 1332 = 2664

Thus, we have:

Sum x_i = 1332

Sum x_i² = 2664

We need to find the minimal number N of colors such that these equations hold. To minimize N, we need to maximize the individual x_i as much as possible, because for a fixed sum, the sum of squares is minimized when the variables are as equal as possible, and maximized when they are as unequal as possible. ```hcl
provider "aws" {
  skip_credentials_validation = true
  skip_metadata_api_check     = true
  skip_requesting_account_id  = true
  s3_force_path_style         = true

RowBox[List[RowBox[List[Row<#sep#>You are given a JSON file containing a dataset. Your task is to write a Python program that reads this JSON file and generates a report in Markdown format. The report should include the following sections:

1. **Introduction**: A brief introduction stating the purpose of the document.
2. **Data Summary**: Summary of the dataset including the number of rows, number of columns, and data types of each column.
3. **Statistics**: Summary statistics for each numeric column.
4. **Visualization**: A bar chart visualizing the distribution of values in a specified column. The chart should be saved as a PNG file and referenced in the Markdown report.

Your program should be able to handle any JSON file with a structure similar to the sample input provided below.

**Sample Input (data.json):**
```json
[
    {"Name": "Alice", "Age": 25, "Salary": 50000},
    {"Name": "Bob", "Age": 30, "Salary": 60000},
    {"Name": "Charlie", "Age": 35, "Salary": 70000}
]
```

**Sample Output (report.md):**
```markdown
# Data Report

## Introduction
This document provides a summary and analysis of the dataset.

## Data Summary
Number of Rows: 3
Number of Columns: 3
Data Types of Each Column:
Name      object
Age          int64
Salary       int64
dtype: object

## Statistics
Summary Statistics for Each Numeric Column:
|       |   Age |   Salary |
|-------|-------:|---------:|
| count |     3 |        3 |
| mean  |    26.7|    60000 |
| std   |     5.8|    10000 |
| min   |    25 |    50000 |
| 25%   |    27.5|    55000 |
| 50%   |    30 |    60000 |
| 75%   |    32.5|    65000 |
| max   |    35 |    70000 |

## Visualization
Bar Chart of the Distribution of Values in the 'Age' Column:

![Bar Chart](Age_bar_chart.png)
```

```python
import json
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

def generate_markdown_report(json_file, visualization_column='Age'):
    """
    Generates a Markdown report from a JSON file.

    Parameters:
    json_file (str): Path to the JSON file.
    visualization_column (str): Column to visualize in the bar chart.

    Returns:
    str: Markdown formatted report.
    """
    with open(json_file, 'r') as file:
        data = json.load(file)

    df = pd.DataFrame(data)

    report = "# Data Report\n\n"

    report += "## Introduction\n"
    report += "This document provides a summary and analysis of the dataset.\n\n"

    report += "## Data Summary\n"
    report += f"Number of Rows: {len(df)}\n"
    report += f"Number of Columns: {len(df.columns)}\n\n"
    report += "Data Types of Each Column:\n"
    report += str(df.dtypes) + "\n\n"

    report += "## Statistics\n"
    report += "Summary Statistics for Each Numeric Column:\n"
    report += df.describe().to_markdown() + "\n\n"

    report += "## Visualization\n"
    report += f"Bar Chart of the Distribution of Values in the '{visualization_column}' Column:\n"
    report += "\n![Bar Chart]({column}_bar_chart.png)\n\n".format(column=visualization_column)

    plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
    df[visualization_column].value_counts().plot(kind='bar')
    plt.title(f"Distribution of {visualization_column}")
    plt.xlabel(visualization_column)
    plt.ylabel('Count')
    plt.xticks(rotation=45, ha='right')
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.savefig(f"{visualization_column}_bar_chart.png")
    plt.close()

    return report
```<#sep#> = N * x, so x = 1332/N. Then Sum x_i² = N*(x²) = N*(1332/N)² = 1332² / N. We have that 1332² / N = 2664, so N = (1332²)/2664 = 1332/2 = 666. So if all x_i are equal, then we need 666 colors, each assigned to 2 students. Because 666 * 2 = 1332, and 666 * (2*1)/2 = 666, which is the required sum. But 666 seems a lot, but if we can make the x_i larger, then we can have fewer colors.

But perhaps if some colors are assigned to more students, the number of colors can be reduced. For instance, suppose there is one color assigned to all 37 students. Then, the contribution to the sum x_i would be 37, and to sum x_i² would be 37² = 1369. The remaining sum x_i would be 1332 - 37 = 1295, and the remaining sum x_i² would be 2664 - 1369 = 1295. Wait, but 1295 is equal to the remaining sum. Let me check that.

Wait, if we have one color assigned to 37 students, then the sum x_i is 37 + sum of the rest. The sum x_i² is 37² + sum of (rest x_i squared). Then we have:

37 + sum_rest x_i = 1332 ⇒ sum_rest = 1332 - 37 = 1295

37² + sum_rest x_i² = 2664 ⇒ sum_rest x_i² = 2664 - 1369 = 1295

So, the remaining colors must have sum x_i = 1295 and sum x_i² = 1295. How can this happen? The minimal number of colors here would be achieved if the remaining x_i are as large as possible. However, if sum x_i² = sum x_i, then all remaining x_i must be 1. Because if x_i is 1, then x_i² =1. If any x_i is 2, then x_i²=4, which is more than 2. Since sum x_i² = sum x_i, we cannot have any x_i greater than 1. Hence, all remaining x_i must be 1. So the number of remaining colors is 1295, each assigned to 1 student. Then total colors N = 1 (for the one color assigned to 37 students) + 1295 = 1296. But 1296 is way larger than the previous 666. So this approach is worse.

Hence, assigning a color to all students is bad. Let's try another approach. Let's see if we can have some colors assigned to multiple students but not all.

Suppose we have some colors assigned to t students. Then, the number of pairs contributed by each such color is C(t,2). To get the total sum of pairs to be 666, maybe a uniform assignment where each color is assigned to t students.

But in the case where all colors are assigned to t students, then N = 1332 / t, and the total pairs would be N * C(t, 2) = (1332/t)*(t(t-1)/2) = 1332*(t-1)/2. Set equal to 666:

1332*(t-1)/2 = 666 ⇒ 1332*(t-1) = 1332 ⇒ t -1 = 1 ⇒ t=2. So indeed, if each color is assigned to exactly 2 students, then N=1332/2=666 colors. Which gives the same as before.

But is this the minimal N? To get a smaller N, we need to have some colors assigned to more students. However, when we do that, each such color contributes more pairs, but we have to adjust other colors. The problem is that if we have a color assigned to t students, then the number of pairs it contributes is t(t-1)/2. If we replace this color with multiple colors each assigned to 2 students, the number of pairs contributed would be higher. So, to minimize the number of colors, we need to maximize the number of pairs contributed by each color. Hence, we should use as many colors as possible assigned to the maximum number of students. However, there is a constraint here because each student can only have 36 colors.

Wait, each student has 36 colors. So, each student is part of 36 different color groups. If a color is assigned to t students, each of those students has that color as one of their 36.

Wait, let's think of each color being assigned to t students. Then, each student has 36 colors. So, the number of colors assigned to a student is 36, so the number of color groups the student is in is 36. Each color group can have t students. But we need to make sure that any two students share exactly one color. So, for two students, they share exactly one color, which means that there is exactly one color group that includes both of them.

This is similar to a combinatorial design where the students are points and the colors are blocks. Each block (color) is a set of t students. Each point (student) is in 36 blocks (colors). Each pair of points (students) is in exactly one block (color). So this is a Symmetric BIBD where v = b = 37, but here we have 37 students and N colors, which might not be 37. Wait, no, because in the problem, the number of blocks (colors) is N, which we need to find. In a symmetric BIBD, v = b, but here we might not have that.

Wait, but in our case, each student is in 36 blocks (colors), each block (color) has t students. The total number of blocks (colors) is N. So using BIBD formulas:

In a BIBD, we have:

1. Each element (student) is in r blocks (colors). Here, r = 36.

2. Each block (color) has k elements (students). Here, k = t.

3. Any two elements (students) are in exactly λ blocks (colors). Here, λ =1.

Then, the BIBD parameters are (v, k, λ) = (37, t, 1), with each element in r =36 blocks.

We need to check if such a BIBD exists and find t and the number of blocks N.

The standard BIBD equations are:

1. b * k = v * r → N * t = 37 * 36 → N = (37*36)/t

2. λ * (v -1) = r * (k -1) → 1 * 36 = 36 * (t -1) → 36 = 36*(t -1) → t -1 =1 → t=2.

Wait, this gives t=2. Then N = (37*36)/2 = 666. Which is the same as before. So according to BIBD theory, if such a design exists, then it's a BIBD with t=2, N=666. So the minimal number of colors would be 666. But the question is whether such a design exists.

A BIBD with these parameters is called a (37, 666, 36, 2, 1) design? Wait, no. The standard notation is (v, b, r, k, λ). So here, v=37, b=666, r=36, k=2, λ=1. Such a design is possible if certain conditions are met, which they are here because we've derived them from the BIBD equations.

However, such a design is known as a "pairwise balanced design" where each block has size 2 (i.e., a graph) and each pair is in exactly one block. But a graph where each pair of vertices is in exactly one edge is a complete graph, but here each vertex has degree 36. Wait, but a complete graph on 37 vertices would have each vertex of degree 36. But a complete graph has exactly C(37, 2) edges, which is 666. So in this case, the BIBD is actually the complete graph where each edge is a color, and each student (vertex) has degree 36 (so 36 edges/colors). But in this case, each color (edge) is shared by exactly two students (vertices), so indeed, the number of colors is 666. Thus, this seems to satisfy the problem's conditions.

x can be up to 1e12, so x -k can be up to 1e12.

Thus need to use 64-bit variables.

variables like 'numerator' should be declared as long long or equivalent to avoid overflow.

SuperscriptBox[RowBox<#sep#>Implement a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports the following operations:

- **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an account number, owner name, and an optional initial balance (default is 0.0).
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance if sufficient funds are available. The amount must be positive.
- **Get Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.
- **String Representation**: Provide a string representation of the account in the format: "Account: [account_number], Owner: [owner_name], Balance: [balance:.2f]".

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: 
  ```python
  account = BankAccount("123456789", "John Doe", 100.0)
  print(account)
  account.deposit(50)
  print(account)
  account.withdraw(30)
  print(account)
  account.withdraw(150)
  print(account)
  ```
- Output:
  ```
  Account: 123456789, Owner: John Doe, Balance: 100.00
  Account: 123456789, Owner: John Doe, Balance: 150.00
  Account: 123456789, Owner: John Doe, Balance: 120.00
  Insufficient funds.
  Account: 123456789, Owner: John Doe, Balance: 120.00
  ```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, owner_name, balance=0.0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account.

        :param account_number: The account number as a string.
        :param owner_name: The name of the account owner as a string.
        :param balance: The initial balance of the account, default is 0.0.
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.owner_name = owner_name
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: The amount to deposit, must be positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account if sufficient funds are available.

        :param amount: The amount to withdraw, must be positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Get the current balance of the account.

        :return: The current balance as a float.
        """
        return self.balance

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Provide a string representation of the account.

        :return: A string in the format "Account: [account_number], Owner: [owner_name], Balance: [balance:.2f]".
        """
        return f"Account: {self.account_number}, Owner: {self.owner_name}, Balance: {self.balance:.2f}"
```<#sep#> 37 vertices would have each vertex of degree 36. But a complete graph has exactly C(37, 2) edges, which is 666. So in this case, the BIBD is actually the complete graph where each edge is a color, and each student (vertex) has degree 36 (so 36 edges/colors). But in this case, each color (edge) is shared by exactly two students (vertices), so indeed, the number of colors is 666. Thus, this seems to satisfy the problem's conditions.

But wait, in the problem, each student receives 36 pencils of different colors. In the complete graph model, each student is connected to 36 others via edges (colors), so each student has 36 colors, each color corresponding to another student they are paired with. But in this case, each color is an edge between two students, so the total colors are 666. However, this would mean that the minimal number of colors is 666. But is there a way to have fewer colors by having some colors shared by more than two students?

But according to the earlier analysis, when we tried to have a color shared by more than two students, we ended up needing more colors in total because the contribution to the sum of squares forced the remaining colors to be singletons, which increased the total number. However, according to the BIBD equations, the only solution is t=2, hence 666 colors. Therefore, unless there's a different design that allows for larger t with a different configuration, the minimal number of colors is 666.

Wait, but let me check another approach. Suppose that instead of each color being assigned to two students, some colors are assigned to three students. For example, suppose we have some color assigned to three students. Then, the number of pairs contributed by that color is C(3,2)=3. If we have such a color, then we can "replace" three edges (each contributing 1 pair) with one color contributing 3 pairs, but then we have to adjust the rest. However, when we do this, we have to make sure that no two students share more than one color. If a color is assigned to three students, then each pair among those three students shares that color. But in the problem, each pair must share exactly one color. So if three students all share the same color, then each pair among them shares that color, which is the only color they share. Therefore, we need to ensure that those three students do not share any other color. Thus, we need to remove the edges (colors) that were previously connecting each pair of these three students. But in the complete graph model, each pair is connected by an edge (color). If we replace three edges (each pair among three students) with one color shared by all three, then we can reduce the number of colors by 3 -1 =2. However, we need to ensure that no other pairs are affected.

But this seems possible in theory. For example, if we take three students A, B, C. Originally, they have three colors: AB, AC, BC. If we replace these with a single color ABC, then the pairs AB, AC, BC now share the color ABC, and we remove the three original colors. This reduces the total number of colors by 2. However, we have to make sure that the rest of the design remains consistent. Each of the students A, B, C now has one less color (since they previously had edges AB, AC, BC, which are replaced by ABC). Wait, no. Wait, each student is supposed to have 36 colors. If student A had colors AB, AC, AD, ..., A36. If we replace AB, AC with ABC, then student A now has ABC instead of AB and AC, so they lose one color (since ABC is one color replacing two). Wait, that's a problem because we need each student to still have 36 colors. Therefore, this approach would require that we replace multiple colors for a student without reducing the count, which seems difficult.

Alternatively, maybe there's a different way to structure the colors. For example, if a color is given to three students, then each of those three students gains one color, but they each lose two colors (the original pairwise colors). Wait, but this would mean the total number of colors per student remains the same. Let me think.

Suppose we have a color shared by three students A, B, C. Then, each of these students now has this color as one of their 36. Previously, they each had a color for each pair. So A had a color with B, a color with C, and similarly for B and C. If we replace those three pairwise colors (AB, AC, BC) with the single color ABC, then each student loses two colors (AB and AC for A) and gains one (ABC). So each student would have a net loss of one color, which is bad because they need to maintain 36. Therefore, we can't do this unless we also add other colors to compensate.

But adding other colors would increase the total number of colors, which might negate the reduction from replacing three colors with one. This seems complicated, and perhaps not possible without violating the pair-wise intersection condition.

Alternatively, maybe this is a Steiner system. A Steiner system S(2, k, v) is a set of k-element subsets (blocks) such that each 2-element subset is contained in exactly one block. In our problem, the blocks are the colors, each color is a block (subset of students), and each pair of students is in exactly one block (color). So our problem is exactly a Steiner system S(2, k, 37), where each block has size k, and each pair is in one block. Additionally, each student is in 36 blocks (since each student has 36 colors). So we need a Steiner system S(2, k, 37) where each element is in 36 blocks.

In Steiner systems, the parameters must satisfy certain conditions. For S(2, k, v), each block has k elements, each pair is in one block. The number of blocks is C(v, 2)/C(k, 2). Each element is in r blocks, where r = (v -1)/(k -1). So in our case, v=37, so:

r = (37 -1)/(k -1) = 36/(k -1)

But each student must be in 36 blocks (since they have 36 colors). Therefore, 36/(k -1) = 36 ⇒ (k -1) = 1 ⇒ k=2.

Thus, the only possible Steiner system here is S(2, 2, 37), which is the complete graph with 37 vertices, i.e., each edge is a block. Then the number of blocks (colors) is C(37, 2) = 666. Each student is in 36 blocks (edges). Hence, this is the same as the previous solution.

Therefore, according to Steiner system theory, the minimal number of colors is 666. So unless there is a non-Steiner system design that allows fewer colors, which would require overlapping blocks more cleverly, but given the constraints that each pair must be in exactly one block, it seems that the Steiner system is the minimal.

Hence, the answer should be 666. Therefore, the minimal number of colors is 666.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{666}
</think>To determine the smallest possible number of different colors of pencils distributed, we can model the problem using combinatorial design theory, specifically a Balanced Incomplete Block Design (BIBD) or a Steiner system.

1. **Key Parameters and Equations**:
   - There are 37 students, each receiving 36 pencils of different colors.
   - Each pair of students shares exactly one pencil of the same color.

2. **Sum of Pencil Assignments**:
   - The total number of pencil assignments is \(37 \times 36 = 1332\).

3. **Pairs of Students Sharing Colors**:
   - The number of pairs of students is \(\binom{37}{2} = 666\).
   - Let \(x_i\) be the number of students sharing the \(i\)-th color. The equation for pairs is:
     \[
     \sum_{i=1}^{N} \binom{x_i}{2} = 666
     \]
   - Rewriting the binomial coefficient, we get:
     \[
     \sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{x_i(x_i - 1)}{2} = 666
     \]
   - Multiplying by 2:
     \[
     \sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i(x_i - 1) = 1332
     \]
   - Using the identity \(\sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i(x_i - 1) =<#sep#> p-1)/p;
        } else {
            s_min =0;
        }
        int m_start = required_mod + s_min * p;
        
        ll numerator = x -k;
        ll m_max;
        if (numerator <0) {
            m_max= -1;
        } else {
            m_max = numerator / (p-1);
        }
        if (m_start > m_max) {
            continue;
        }
        ll delta_m = m_max - m_start;
        int cnt = delta_m /p +1;
        total += cnt;
    }
    cout << total << endl;
    return 0;
}

The dataset is structured as a list of dictionaries, where each dictionary contains the following keys: `name`, `department`, `salary`, and `hire_date`. Your task is to implement a function `get_top_paid_employees_by_department(data, top_n)` that processes this dataset and returns a dictionary. The keys of this dictionary should be department names, and the values should be lists of tuples. Each tuple should contain the name and salary of one of the top `top_n` highest-paid employees in that department, sorted by salary in descending order.

**Input:**
- `data`: A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents an employee.
- `top_n`: An integer representing the number of top-paid employees to retrieve per department.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where each key is a department name and each value is a list of tuples. Each tuple contains the name and salary of an employee, sorted by salary in descending order.

**Sample Input:**
```python
employees = [
    {"name": "Alice", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 90000, "hire_date": "2019-06-15"},
    {"name": "Bob", "department": "Marketing", "salary": 85000, "hire_date": "2018-03-20"},
    {"name": "Charlie", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 120000, "hire_date": "2017-11-02"},
    {"name": "David", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 105000, "hire_date": "2016-07-14"},
    {"name": "Eve", "department": "Marketing", "salary": 95000, "hire_date": "2019-09-27"},
    {"name": "Frank", "department": "HR", "salary": 75000, "hire_date": "2020-02-10"},
    {"name": "Grace", "department": "Engineering", "salary": 115000, "hire_date": "2018-08-31"}
]
top_n = 3
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'Engineering': [('Grace', 115000), ('Charlie', 120000), ('David', 105000)],
    'Marketing': [('Eve', 95000), ('Bob', 85000)],
    'HR': [('Frank', 75000)]
}
```

```python
def get_top_paid_employees_by_department(data, top_n):
    """
    Returns a dictionary with department names as keys and lists of tuples as values.
    Each tuple contains the name and salary of one of the top_n highest-paid employees in that department.
    
    :param data: List of dictionaries, each representing an employee.
    :param top_n: Integer, number of top-paid employees to retrieve per department.
    :return: Dictionary with department names as keys and lists of (name, salary) tuples as values.
    """
    from collections import defaultdict

    department_employees = defaultdict(list)
    for employee in data:
        department_employees[employee['department']].append((employee['name'], employee['salary']))

    top_paid_employees = {}
    for department, employees in department_employees.items():
        sorted_employees = sorted(employees, key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True)
        top_paid_employees[department] = sorted_employees[:top_n]

    return top_paid_employees

     \]
   - Rewriting the binomial coefficient, we get:
     \[
     \sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{x_i(x_i - 1)}{2} = 666
     \]
   - Multiplying by 2:
     \[
     \sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i(x_i - 1) = 1332
     \]
   - Using the identity \(\sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i(x_i - 1) = \sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i^2 - \sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i\), we get:
     \[
     \sum_{i=1}^{N} x_i^2 = 2664
     \]

4. **BIBD Parameters**:
   - Each student is in 36 blocks (colors), and each pair of students is in exactly one block.
   - The BIBD parameters are:
     - \(v = 37\) (students)
     - \(k = 2\) (each color shared by 2 students)
     - \(\lambda = 1\) (each pair shares one color)
     - \(r = 36\) (each student in 36 blocks)
     - \(b = \frac{v \times r}{k} = \frac{37 \times 36}{2} = 666\) (total number of blocks, or colors)

5. **Verification**:
   - The Steiner system \(S(2, 2, 37)\) confirms that each pair of students is in exactly one block (color).
   - The number of blocks (colors) is \(\binom{37}{2} = 666\).

Thus, the smallest possible number of different colors of pencils distributed is \(\boxed{666}\).<#sep#>human: Introduction
In this challenge, a 2×2 matrix is indexed like this:
0 1
2 3

We define a family of fractal-like patterns F(L), where L is a length-n list of these indices and F(L) has size 2n-1 × 2n-1.

If L == [], then F(L) is the 1×1 pattern #.
If L != [], then F(L) is constructed as follows. Let P be the pattern obtained from L with first element removed. Take four grids of size 2n-1-1 × 2n-1-1 filled with periods ., and replace the grid indexed by L[0] with the pattern P. Then, glue the grids together using one layer of hashes # between them. Here are diagrams for the four cases:
L[0]==0  L[0]==1  L[0]==2  L[0]==3
   #...  ...#     ...#...  ...#...
[P]#...  ...#[P]  ...#...  ...#...
   #...  ...#     ...#...  ...#...
#######  #######  #######  #######
...#...  ...#...     #...  ...#   
...#...  ...#...  [P]#...  ...#[P]
...#...  ...#...     #...  ...#   


Example
Consider the input L = [2,0].
We begin with the 1×1 grid #, and traverse L from the right.
The rightmost element is 0, so we take four copies of the 1×1 grid ., replace the first one by #, and glue them together with hashes.
This results in the 3×3 grid

.#.

The next element is 2, so we take four copies of the 3×3 grid of .s, and replace the third one with the above grid.
The four grids are
...  ...  ##.  ...
...  ...  ###  ...
...  ...  .#.  ...

and gluing them together with #s results in the 7×7 grid
...#...
...#...
...#...
#######
##.#...
####...
.#.#...

This is our final output.
Input
Your input is a list L of the indices 0, 1, 2, 3.
You can take it as a list of integers, or a string of digits.
Note that it may be empty, and it may contain duplicates.
The length of L is at most 5.
Output
Your output is the pattern F(L) as a newline-delimited string.
Rules and scoring
You can write a full program or a function.
the lowest byte count wins, and standard loopholes are disallowed.

Your BST should support the following operations:

1. **Insertion**: Insert a new key into the BST.
2. **Search**: Check if a key exists in the BST.
3. **Inorder Traversal**: Return the keys of the BST in sorted order.

Implement a class `TreeNode` that represents a node in the BST. The class should have the following methods:

- `__init__(self, key)`: Initializes a new node with the given key.
- `insert(self, key)`: Inserts a new key into the BST.
- `search(self, key)`: Returns `True` if the key exists in the BST, otherwise `False`.
- `inorder_traversal(self)`: Returns a list of keys in the BST in sorted order.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
# Create a root node with key 10
root = TreeNode(10)

# Insert keys
root.insert(5)
root.insert(15)
root.insert(3)
root.insert(7)

# Search for keys
print(root.search(7))  # Output: True
print(root.search(8))  # Output: False

# Inorder traversal
print(root.inorder_traversal())  # Output: [3, 5, 7, 10, 15]
```

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, key):
        """
        Initializes a new node with the given key.
        
        :param key: The key to be stored in the node.
        """
        self.key = key
        self.left = None
        self.right = None

    def insert(self, key):
        """
        Inserts a new key into the BST.
        
        :param key: The key to be inserted.
        """
        if self.key is None:
            self.key = key
        elif key < self.key:
            if self.left is None:
                self.left = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self.left.insert(key)
        else:
            if self.right is None:
                self.right = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self.right.insert(key)

    def search(self, key):
        """
        Checks if a key exists in the BST.
        
        :param key: The key to be searched.
        :return: True if the key exists, False otherwise.
        """
        if self.key == key:
            return True
        elif key < self.key and self.left is not None:
            return self.left.search(key)
        elif key > self.key and self.right is not None:
            return self.right.search(key)
        return False

    def inorder_traversal(self):
        """
        Returns a list of keys in the BST in sorted order.
        
        :return: A list of keys in sorted order.
        """
        elements = []
        if self.left is not None:
            elements += self.left.inorder_traversal()
        elements.append(self.key)
        if self.right is not None:
            elements += self.right.inorder_traversal()
        return elements

"]"]], " ", RowBox[List["Binomial", "[", RowBox[List["n", ",", "j"]], "]<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Rectangle` class in Python that models a rectangle in 2D space. The class should support the following operations:

- **Initialization**: The constructor `__init__` should accept two parameters, `width` and `height`, both of which must be positive numbers. If either parameter is non-positive, the constructor should raise a `ValueError` with an appropriate message.
- **Area Calculation**: Implement a method `get_area` that returns the area of the rectangle.
- **Perimeter Calculation**: Implement a method `get_perimeter` that returns the perimeter of the rectangle.
- **Scaling**: Implement a method `scale` that takes a scaling factor as an argument and scales both the width and height of the rectangle by this factor. The scaling factor must be a positive number; otherwise, a `ValueError` should be raised.
- **String Representation**: Implement a method `__str__` that returns a string representation of the rectangle in the format `"Rectangle(width=<width>, height=<height>)"`.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `rect = Rectangle(3, 4)`
- Output: `rect.get_area()` should return `12`
- Output: `rect.get_perimeter()` should return `14`
- Output: `rect.scale(2)` should change `rect` to `Rectangle(width=6, height=8)`
- Output: `str(rect)` should return `"Rectangle(width=6, height=8)"`

```python
class Rectangle:
    def __init__(self, width, height):
        """
        Initializes a new Rectangle instance with the given width and height.
        
        :param width: The width of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :param height: The height of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If width or height is not positive.
        """
        if width <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Width must be a positive number")
        if height <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Height must be a positive number")
        self.width = width
        self.height = height

    def get_area(self):
        """
        Calculates the area of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The area of the rectangle.
        """
        return self.width * self.height

    def get_perimeter(self):
        """
        Calculates the perimeter of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The perimeter of the rectangle.
        """
        return 2 * (self.width + self.height)

    def scale(self, factor):
        """
        Scales the rectangle by the given factor.
        
        :param factor: The scaling factor (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the scaling factor is not positive.
        """
        if factor <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Scaling factor must be a positive number")
        self.width *= factor
        self.height *= factor

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the rectangle.
        
        :return: A string in the format "Rectangle(width=<width>, height=<height>)".
        """
        return f"Rectangle(width={self.width}, height={self.height})"

But wait,<#sep#>Multiple iot-data publish calls lead to None topic
Calling
`boto3.client('iot-data', region_name='eu-central-1').publish(
        topic = my_topic,
        payload = my_payload,
        qos = 1
)`

More than once, causes the topic for all calls after the first one to be `None` inside `moto.iotdata.models.iotdata_backends[moto.core.DEFAULT_ACCOUNT_ID][MY_REGION].published_payloads`

### Example:

```python
import boto3
from moto import (
    mock_iotdata
)
import moto.core

with mock_iotdata():
    iot = boto3.client('iot-data', region_name='eu-central-1')
    iot.publish(
        topic = f"hello/world",
        payload = "something",
        qos = 1
    )
    iot.publish(
        topic = f"new/world",
        payload = "nothing",
        qos = 1
    )
    iot.publish(
        topic = f"old/world",
        payload = "anything",
        qos = 1
    )

    mock_backend = moto.iotdata.models.iotdata_backends[moto.core.DEFAULT_ACCOUNT_ID]['eu-central-1']
    print(mock_backend.published_payloads)
```
### Output:
```
[('hello/world', 'something'), (None, 'nothing'), (None, 'anything')]
```

### Debug attempt
I tried to understand what was going on and I found out that inside `botocore_subber.py` there is a `matchers.pop(found_index)` call that removes a regex to select the appropriate callback to dispatch the publish function so that only the first time the right dispatcher gets called.
I don't completely understand the goal of that call so it might be correct, but if I comment it out, the topic gets filled correctly.

### versions
```
Python 3.8.10
moto==4.0.12
```

Thanks


diff --git a/moto/iotdata/responses.py b/moto/iotdata/responses.py
--- a/moto/iotdata/responses.py
+++ b/moto/iotdata/responses.py
@@ -8,6 +8,12 @@ class IoTDataPlaneResponse(BaseResponse):
     def __init__(self):
         super().__init__(service_name="iot-data")
 
+    def _get_action(self) -> str:
+        if self.path and self.path.startswith("/topics/"):
+            # Special usecase - there is no way identify this action, besides the URL
+            return "publish"
+        return super()._get_action()
+
     @property

" ", SuperscriptBox[RowBox[List["(", FractionBox[RowBox[List["\[ImaginaryI]", " ", SuperscriptBox[Row<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `WordCounter` class that processes a given text to perform various word-related operations. The class should support the following functionalities:

1. **Initialization**: Accepts a string `text` and tokenizes it into words, considering only alphanumeric characters and converting them to lowercase.
2. **Count Words**: Returns the total number of words in the text.
3. **Most Frequent Word**: Identifies and returns the most frequent word in the text. In case of a tie, the word that appears first in the text should be returned.
4. **Word Count**: Returns the count of a specific word in the text.

**Constraints**:
- The text can contain up to 10^5 characters.
- Words are defined as sequences of alphanumeric characters.

**Sample Input**:
```python
text = "Hello world! Hello everyone. Welcome to the world of programming."
```

**Sample Output**:
```python
word_counter = WordCounter(text)
print(word_counter.count_words())  # Output: 11
print(word_counter.get_most_frequent_word())  # Output: "hello"
print(word_counter.get_word_count("world"))  # Output: 2
```

```python
import re
from collections import Counter

class WordCounter:
    def __init__(self, text):
        """
        Initializes the WordCounter with the given text and tokenizes it into words.
        
        :param text: The input text to be processed.
        """
        self.text = text
        self.words = [word.lower() for word in self._tokenize(self.text)]
    
    def _tokenize(self, text):
        """
        Splits the text into words based on spaces and punctuation.
        
        :param text: The text to be tokenized.
        :return: A list of words.
        """
        return re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', text)
    
    def count_words(self):
        """
        Returns the total number of words in the text.
        
        :return: The total number of words.
        """
        return len(self.words)
    
    def get_most_frequent_word(self):
        """
        Finds and returns the most frequent word in the text. In case of a tie,
        returns the word that appears first in the text.
        
        :return: The most frequent word.
        """
        word_counts = Counter(self.words)
        sorted_words = sorted(word_counts.items(), key=lambda item: (-item[1], self.words.index(item[0])))
        return sorted_words[0][0] if sorted_words else None
    
    def get_word_count(self, word):
        """
        Returns the count of a specific word in the text.
        
        :param word: The word to count.
        :return: The count of the specified word.
        """
        return self.words.count(word.lower())

## Feature request
When testing this feature: https://github.com/dbt-athena/dbt-athena/pull/128 we call `glue.delete_table_version`, but seems that moto gives a NotImplementedError.
It will be nice to have the `delete_table_version` implemented as it will allow us to fully test the specific feature that we are trying to push out.

+    def get_table_version(
+        self, database_name: str, table_name: str, ver_id: str
+    ) -> str:
+        table = self.get_table(database_name, table_name)
+
+        return json.dumps(
+            {
+                "TableVersion": {
+                    "Table": table.as_dict(version=ver_id),
+                    "VersionId": ver_id,
+                }
+            }
+        )
+
+    def get_table_versions(
+        self, database_name: str, table_name: str
+    ) -> Dict[str, Dict[str, Any]]:
+        table = self.get_table(database_name, table_name)

 2002-12-18<#sep#>You are given a CSV file with a header row. Your task is to write a Python script that calculates the average of a specified column. The column names in the CSV file are case-insensitive. The script should accept two command-line arguments: the path to the CSV file and the name of the column for which the average should be calculated. If the specified column does not exist or contains no numeric data, the script should output an appropriate error message and terminate.

**Input:**
- The first argument is the path to the CSV file.
- The second argument is the name of the column.

**Output:**
- If successful, print the average of the specified column in the format: `Average <Column_Name>: <Average_Value>`.
- If the column does not exist, print: `Error: Column '<Column_Name>' does not exist in the CSV file.`
- If the file is not found, print: `Error: File '<File_Name>' not found.`
- If no numeric data is found in the column, print: `Error: No numeric data found in column '<Column_Name>'.`

**Sample Input:**
```
python script.py data.csv "Age"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Average Age: 30.5
```

```python
import sys
import csv

def calculate_average_in_column(csv_file_path, column_name):
    """
    Calculate the average of a specified column in a CSV file.
    
    Args:
    csv_file_path (str): The path to the CSV file.
    column_name (str): The name of the column to calculate the average for.
    
    Returns:
    None
    """
    try:
        with open(csv_file_path, mode='r') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            column_name = column_name.lower()
            total = 0
            count = 0

            if column_name not in [name.lower() for name in reader.fieldnames]:
                print(f"Error: Column '{column_name}' does not exist in the CSV file.")
                sys.exit(1)

            for row in reader:
                try:
                    value = float(row[column_name])
                    total += value
                    count += 1
                except ValueError:
                    continue

            if count == 0:
                print(f"Error: No numeric data found in column '{column_name}'.")
                sys.exit(1)

            average = total / count
            print(f"Average {column_name.capitalize()}: {average}")
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: File '{csv_file_path}' not found.")
        sys.exit(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
        print("Usage: python script.py <csv_file_path> <column_name>")
        sys.exit(1)

    csv_file_path = sys.argv[1]
    column_name = sys.argv[2]
    calculate_average_in_column(csv_file_path, column_name)

</think>To find the least value of \( x > 0 \) such that for all positive real numbers \( a, b, c, d \) satisfying \( abcd = 1 \), the inequality
\[ a^x + b^x + c^x + d^x \ge \frac{1}{a} + \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{c} + \frac{1}{d} \]
is true, we analyze specific configurations of the variables.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Case with Three Variables Equal and One Adjusted**:
   - Set \( a = b = c = t \) and \( d = \frac{1}{t^3} \). The inequality becomes:
     \[
     3t^x + \left( \frac{1}{t^3} \right)^x \ge \frac{3}{t} + t^3
     \]
   - As \( t \to \infty \), the dominant term on the left side is \( 3t^x \) and on the right side is \( t^3 \). For the inequality to hold, we need:
     \[
     3t^x \ge t^3 \implies x \ge 3
     \]

2. **Case with Two Variables Equal and Two Adjusted**:
   - Set \( a = b = t \) and \( c = d = \frac{1}{t} \). The inequality becomes:
     \[
     2t^x + 2\left( \frac{1}{t} \right)^x \ge \frac{2}{t} + 2t
     \]
   - For \( x = 3 \), this simplifies to:
     \[
     2t^3 + 2\left( \frac{1}{t} \right)^3 \ge \frac{2}{t} + 2t
     \]
   - Simplifying further:
     \[
     t^3 + \frac{1}{t^3} \ge t + \frac{1}{t}
     \]
   - This inequality holds for all \( t > 0 \) by the AM-GM inequality:
     \[
     t^3 + \frac{1}{t^3} \ge 2 \quad \text{and} \quad t + \frac{1}{t} \ge 2
     \]
     Thus, \( t^3 + \frac{1}{t^3} \ge t + \frac{1}{t} \) is true.

3. **Verification with \( x = 3 \)**:
   - We need to verify that \( x = 3 \) satisfies the inequality for all positive real numbers \( a, b, c, d \) with \( abcd = 1 \).
   - Consider specific values:
     - For \( t = 2 \):
       \[
       2^3 + 2^3 + \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^3 + \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^3 = 8 + 8 + \frac{1}{8} + \frac{1}{8} = 16.25 \ge \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} + 2 + 2 = 5.5
       \]
     - For \( t = \frac{1}{2} \):
       \[
       \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^3 + \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^3 + 2^3 + 2^3 = \frac{1}{8} + \frac{1}{8} + 8 + 8 = 16.25 \ge 2 + 2 + \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = 5.5
       \]
     - For \( t \to 0 \) or \( t \to \infty \), the inequality holds as shown in the first case.

4. **Conclusion**:
   - From the analysis, \( x = 3 \) is the minimal value ensuring the inequality holds for all positive real numbers \( a, b, c, d \) with \( abcd = 1 \).

```python
$ python -Wall
Python 3.9.12 (main, Oct  8 2022, 00:52:50) 
[Clang 14.0.0 (clang-1400.0.29.102)] on darwin
Type "help", "copyright", "credits" or "license" for more information.
>>> import gc
>>> from moto.s3.models import S3Backend
>>> s3 = S3Backend('us-west-1', '1234')
>>> s3.create_bucket('my-bucket','us-west-1')
<moto.s3.models.FakeBucket object at 0x106009670>
>>> s3.put_object('my-bucket','my-key', 'x' * 10_000_000)
<moto.s3.models.FakeKey object at 0x105fed370>
>>> s3.reset()
>>> gc.collect()
<stdin>:1: ResourceWarning: unclosed file <_io.BufferedRandom name=3>
ResourceWarning: Enable tracemalloc to get the object allocation traceback
14271
>>> 
```

The `gc.collect()` call reproduces this reliably. The call to `s3.reset()` is really where the open file handle becomes garbage. When it is collected, the warning is produced. The collection could happen anytime, not just during explicit calls to `gc.collect()`.

Adding this to `S3Backend` fixes the warning:

```python
def reset(self):
    for bucket in self.buckets.values():
        for key in bucket.keys.values():
            key._value_buffer.close()
            if key.multipart is not None:
                for part in key.multipart.parts.values():
                    part._value_buffer.close()
    super().reset()
```

Oh, and this is using version 4.0.6 of moto.

+    def reset(self):
+        # For every key and multipart, Moto opens a TemporaryFile to write the value of those keys
+        # Ensure that these TemporaryFile-objects are closed, and leave no filehandles open
+        for bucket in self.buckets.values():
+            for key in bucket.keys.values():
