<#sep#>Home | ENGINEERING | Two-degrees-of-freedom linear system response of structures

# Two-degrees-of-freedom linear system response of structures

Author :  S. Rajasekaran    Posted On :  27.08.2016 09:57 am

Abstract: This chapter deals with the free and forced vibration of two-degrees-of-freedom systems. Coordinate coupling is illustrated by means of an example. The concept behind the vibration absorber is given.

Two-degrees-of-freedom linear system response of structures

Abstract: This chapter deals with the free and forced vibration of two-degrees-of-freedom systems. Coordinate coupling is illustrated by means of an example. The concept behind the vibration absorber is given.

Key words: beat, dynamic coupling, static coupling, vibration absorber, positive definite.

Overview

In many textbooks the concepts presented in the previous chapters are extended from a single-degree-of-freedom (SDOF) system to a multiple-degrees-of-freedom (MDOF) system without first presenting the solutions for the two-degrees-of-freedom system. Such an approach does not allow readers to gradually learn the methodology and also means many important structural engineering concepts are not presented. Therefore, this page is devoted to the two-degrees-of-freedom system.

When a system requires two coordinates to describe its motion, it is said to have two degrees of freedom. A two-degrees-of-freedom system will have two natural frequencies when free vibration takes place at one of these natural frequencies, a definite relationship exists between the amplitude of the two coordinates and the configuration is referred to as normal mode. The two-degrees-of-freedom system will then have two natural modes of vibrations corresponding to the two natural frequencies. For a linear system, free vibration initiated under any condition will in general be the two normal modes of vibration.

Free vibration of a two-degrees-of-freedom system will be taken first, and then its forced vibration characteristics dealt with. There are many ways that a two-degrees-of-freedom system can be represented, three of which are shown in Fig. 9.1.

Free vibration of undamped two-degrees-of-freedom system

The system shown in Fig. 9.2 has two masses m1 and m2 connected by springs of stiffness k1, and k2. It is a two-degrees-of-freedom system and the configura-tion is fully described by two displacements x1 and x2 as shown in Fig. 9.2.

Writing the dynamic equations of equilibrium, considering the free body diagrams shown in Fig. 9.3, we get

m ]{ X } + [ k ]{ X= {0}                    9.3

One can identify [m] as mass matrix and [k] as stiffness matrix. {m} and [k] are symmetric and positive definite. Mass matrix is uncoupled and stiffness matrix is coupled and hence it is known as dynamically uncoupled and statically coupled system.

Try a solution

X1 = A1 sin (ωn+ φ); X2 = A2 sin (ωn+ φ)      9.4

Equation 9.6 involves two homogeneous equations. A trivial solution is A1 = A2 = 0. Equation 9.6 is rewritten as

For a non-trivial solution to exist, the determinant of the above matrix should be equal to zero. Simplification yields the characteristic equation as

Then the system vibrates in first mode as shown in Fig. 9.4. In the first normal mode, two masses move in phase as shown in Fig. 9.4 and the centre spring is not compressed.

X1 = A1 sin (ωn2+ φ2); X2 = A1 sin (ωn2+ φ2)         9.19

The second mode is also plotted in Fig. 9.4. In the second normal mode, the masses move in opposition, out of phase with each other. For the second mode there is one node, which is the point other than support at which displacement is zero. For the

%two degrees of freedom forced vibration undamped clc;

mass matrix

m=[1 0;0 1];

%stiffness matrix k=[5 -3;-3 5];

%give initial displacements and velocities u=[1 0 0 0];

%calculate flexibility matrix

a=inv(k);

am=a*m;

%find eigen values and eigenvectors [ev,evu]=eig(am);

%calculate frequencies and factors for i=1:2

omega(i)=sqrt(1/evu(i,i));

mew(i)=ev(i,2)/ev(i,1); end

%find b matrix

b=[0 1 0 1;omega(1) 0 omega(2) 0;0 mew(1) 0 mew(2);...

omega(1)*mew(1) 0 omega(2)*mew(2) 0]; a=inv(b)*u’;

for i=1:401 tt(i)=(i-1)*.02; t=tt(i);

bb=[sin(omega(1)*t) cos(omega(1)*t) sin(omega(2)*t) cos(omega(2)*t); o m e g a ( 1 ) * c o s ( o m e g a ( 1 ) * t ) - o m e g a ( 1 ) * s i n ( o m e g a ( 1 ) * t ) omega(2)*cos(omega(2)*t)...

u(i)=c(1);

v(i)=c(3); end

Program 9.3: MATLAB program to solve coupled differential equations

MATLAB has several functions or solvers, based on the use of Runge–Kutta methods that can be used for the solution of a system of first order ordinary differential equations. It is to be noted that nth order ordinary differential equations are converted to n first order ordinary differential equations before using the MATLAB functions. The MATLAB function ode23 implements a combination of second and third order Runge–Kutta methods while the function ode45 is based on a combination of fourth and fifth order Runge–Kutta methods. To solve a system of first order differential equation y˙ = f ( t , y) using MATLAB function ode23, the following command can be used

[t,y]=ode23[‘dfunc’,tspan,y0]

where ‘dfunc’ is the name of the function m-file whose input must be t and and whose output must be a column vector denoting dy/don f (t, y). The number of rows in the column vector must be equal to the number of first order equations. The vector ‘tspan’ should contain the initial and final values of the independent variable t, and optimally any intermediate value of ‘t’ at which the solution is desired. The vector y<#sep#>You are given a CSV file named `data.csv` containing information about students. Each row in the CSV file represents a student and includes the following fields: `id`, `name`, `age`, and `grades`. The `grades` field is a comma-separated list of numerical values representing the student's grades. Your task is to write a Python program that reads this file, calculates the average grade for each student, and identifies the student with the highest average grade. The program should output the ID, name, and average grade for each student, followed by the ID, name, and average grade of the student with the highest average grade.

**Input Format:**
- The CSV file `data.csv` is formatted as follows:
  ```
  id,name,age,grades
  1,Alice,20,85,90,78
  2,Bob,22,88,92,81
  3,Charlie,21,70,75,80
  ```

**Output Format:**
- For each student, print their ID, name, and average grade.
- Print the ID, name, and average grade of the student with the highest average grade.
- If there are no valid students or all students have invalid grade data, print an appropriate message.

**Sample Input:**
```
id,name,age,grades
1,Alice,20,85,90,78
2,Bob,22,88,92,81
3,Charlie,21,70,75,80
```

**Sample Output:**
```
ID: 1, Name: Alice, Average Grade: 84.33
ID: 2, Name: Bob, Average Grade: 87.00
ID: 3, Name: Charlie, Average Grade: 75.00

Student with the highest average grade: ID: 2, Name: Bob, Average Grade: 87.00
```

```python
import csv

def read_csv_file(file_name):
    """Reads a CSV file and returns a list of dictionaries representing students."""
    students = []
    try:
        with open(file_name, mode='r', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            for row in reader:
                students.append(row)
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file '{file_name}' was not found.")
    except csv.Error as e:
        print(f"Error reading CSV file: {e}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")
    return students

def calculate_average_grade(grades):
    """Calculates the average grade from a comma-separated string of numerical values."""
    try:
        grades_list = list(map(float, grades.split(',')))
        return sum(grades_list) / len(grades_list)
    except ValueError:
        print("Error: Grades should be numerical values separated by commas.")
        return None

def process_students(students):
    """Processes the list of students, calculates average grades, and prints the results."""
    highest_average = -1
    top_student = None
    
    for student in students:
        avg_grade = calculate_average_grade(student['grades'])
        
        if avg_grade is not None:
            print(f"ID: {student['id']}, Name: {student['name']}, Average Grade: {avg_grade:.2f}")
            
            if avg_grade > highest_average:
                highest_average = avg_grade
                top_student = student
    
    if top_student:
        print(f"\nStudent with the highest average grade: ID: {top_student['id']}, Name: {top_student['name']}, Average Grade: {highest_average:.2f}")
    else:
        print("\nNo students were processed or all students had invalid grade data.")

def main():
    """Main function to execute the program."""
    file_name = "data.csv"
    students = read_csv_file(file_name)
    process_students(students)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```<#sep#>The angle formed by the tangents to the curve $y=x^2$ from the point $(r,s)$ in Quadrant $II$ is bisected by the line through $(r,s)$ with slope $1$. Compute the value of $s$.

First, Quadrant II means that the point (r, s) has r negative and s positive. So, r < 0 and s > 0. The problem mentions tangents to the parabola y = x² from the point (r, s). So, I need to find the equations of the tangent lines from (r, s) to the parabola. Then, the angle between these two tangents is bisected by the line with slope 1 passing through (r, s). The goal is to find s.

Let me recall that the equation of a tangent to the parabola y = x² at a point (a, a²) is given by y = 2a(x) - a². This is because the derivative dy/dx = 2x, so at x = a, the slope is 2a. Therefore, the equation of the tangent line is y - a² = 2a(x - a), which simplifies to y = 2a x - 2a² + a² = 2a x - a².

Now, since the tangent line passes through the point (r, s), substituting x = r and y = s into the tangent equation should satisfy it. So:

s = 2a r - a².

This gives us a quadratic equation in terms of a:

a² - 2r a + s = 0.

The solutions to this quadratic will give the values of a where the tangents from (r, s) touch the parabola. Let's denote these two points of tangency as a₁ and a₂. So, using the quadratic formula:

Therefore, the two values of a are a₁ = r + √(r² - s) and a₂ = r - √(r² - s). For real tangents, the discriminant must be positive, so 4r² - 4s ≥ 0 ⇒ r² - s ≥ 0 ⇒ s ≤ r². The Organizations close_account API was added in #5040.

It doesn't match the spec of the public [CloseAccount](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/organizations/latest/APIReference/API_CloseAccount.html) API.

> If the action is successful, the service sends back an HTTP 200 response with an empty HTTP body.

> While the close account request is in progress, Account status will indicate PENDING_CLOSURE. When the close account request completes, the status will change to SUSPENDED. 

The current implementation does neither of those things:

Here's the current implementation:

```python
class FakeAccount:
    ...
    @property
    def close_account_status(self):
        return {
            "CloseAccountStatus": {
                "Id": self.create_account_status_id,
                "AccountName": self.name,
                "State": "SUCCEEDED",
                "RequestedTimestamp": unix_time(datetime.datetime.utcnow()),
                "CompletedTimestamp": unix_time(datetime.datetime.utcnow()),
                "AccountId": self.id,
            }
        }

class OrganizationsBackend:
    ...
    def close_account(self, **kwargs):
        for account_index in range(len(self.accounts)):
            if kwargs["AccountId"] == self.accounts[account_index].id:
                closed_account_status = self.accounts[
                    account_index
                ].close_account_status
                del self.accounts[account_index]
                return closed_account_status

        raise AccountNotFoundException
```

The current implementation returns a CloseAccountStatus object. This does not exist in the public API. Indeed, the real CloseAccount API returns nothing.

The current implementation deletes the account from the backend's account list. That's wrong because the account should remain in the organization in a SUSPENDED state.

I caught this while adding type hints to the organizations models. There is no CloseAccountResponseTypeDef in mypy_boto3_organizations!

<#sep#>23, the following command can be used

[t,y]=ode23[‘dfunc’,tspan,y0]

where ‘dfunc’ is the name of the function m-file whose input must be t and and whose output must be a column vector denoting dy/don f (t, y). The number of rows in the column vector must be equal to the number of first order equations. The vector ‘tspan’ should contain the initial and final values of the independent variable t, and optimally any intermediate value of ‘t’ at which the solution is desired. The vector y0 should contain the initial values of y(t). The function ‘m-file’ should have two input arguments t and y. A similar procedure can be used with MATLAB functions ode45.

Assume y1y3 represent the displacements x1 and x2 and the two second order equations can be converted to four first order equations as

The problem discussed in MATHEMATICA is solved using MATLAB. Figure 9.7 shows the displacement response.

%Matlab program for solving coupled differential equation using RK method tspan=[0:.01:20];

%Initial displacements and velocities y0=[1.0;0.0;0.0;0.0];

%Use RK method of order 4 and 5 combined [t,y]=ode45(‘dfunc1’,tspan,y0);

subplot(211)

plot(t,y(:,1)); xlabel(‘t’); ylabel(‘x1(t)’);

title(‘ x1(t) vs t’); subplot(212) plot(t,y(:,3)); xlabel(‘t’); ylabel(‘x2(t)’); title(‘x2(t) vs t’);

function f=dfunc1(t,y)

% four first order equations are given by f f=zeros(4,1);

%mass matrix m=[1,0;0,1]; %stiffness matrix k=[5 -3;-3 5];

%four first order equations f(1)=y(2);

f(2)=-k(1,1)*y(1)/m(1,1)-k(1,2)*y(3)/m(1,1); f(3)=y(4); f(4)=-k(2,1)*y(1)/m(2,2)-k(2,2)*y(3)/m(2,2);

Example 9.2

Assume k1 = k2 = 1.7; k3 = 0.3. Investigate the beating phenomenon assuming the initial conditions as X1(0) = 1: X1 (0) X2(0) = = 0 .

The beating phenomenon is shown in Fig. 9.8 and the curves are obtained using the MATLAB package. The resulting motion is a rapid oscillation with

a slowly varying period and is referred as beat. Sometimes the two sinusoids add to each other, and all other times they cancel each other out, resulting in a beating phenomenon. The beating phenomenon often manifests itself in mechanical equipment with the emitted sound having a similar cyclically varying magnitude.

Coordinate coupling

The differential equations of motion for the two-degrees-of-freedom system are in general coupled. In that both coordinates appear in each equation. In the most general case, the two equations of the undamped system have the following form:

In the above equations, the mass and stiffness matrices are non-diagonal. Hence mass or dynamic coupling and stiffness or static coupling exist.

It is also possible to find the coordinate system which has neither form of coupling. The two equations are then decoupled and each equation may be solved independently of the other. For example {X} is written in terms of some other coordinate as

{Y} coordinates are called principal coordinates or normal coordinates. Although it is possible to decouple the equations of motion for an undamped

system, this is not the case for the damped system. The following system of equations show uncoupled mass and stiffness matrices but coupled damping matrix.

If in Eq. 9.35 c12 = c21 = 0 then damping is said to be proportional damping. The damping matrix is also uncoupled when we assume damping is proportional to stiffness and mass matrices.

Simple system: two storey shear building

1.   Influence coefficient method

The influence coefficients associated with stiffness and mass matrices are respectively known as stiffness and inertia influence coefficients. The inverse of stiffness matrix is the flexibility matrix. kij is the stiffness influence coefficient defined as the force developed at ‘i’ due to unit force applied at ‘j’. We first formulate the equation of motion for the simplest possible two-degrees-of-freedom system, a highly idealized two storey frame. In this idealization, the beams and floor systems are rigid (infinitely stiff) in flexure. Axial deformations in the beams and columns and the effect of axial force on stiffness of the columns are neglected. Even though it is unrealistic nevertheless we can illustrate how equations of motion for an MDOF system are developed. Consider a two storey frame shown in Fig. 9.15.

The second mode shape is plotted<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports basic operations such as deposit, withdraw, and check balance. The class should handle errors to prevent the account balance from going negative. The `BankAccount` class should have the following attributes: `account_number`, `account_holder`, and `balance`. Initialize these attributes in the constructor with default values of `None`, `None`, and `0`, respectively. Implement methods for depositing and withdrawing money, and a method to check the current balance.

**Input:**
- The `BankAccount` class will be instantiated with `account_number`, `account_holder`, and `balance`.
- The `deposit` method will be called with a positive integer `amount`.
- The `withdraw` method will be called with a positive integer `amount`.

**Output:**
- The `deposit` method should print the deposited amount and the new balance.
- The `withdraw` method should print the withdrawn amount and the new balance, or raise a `ValueError` if the withdrawal amount exceeds the balance.
- The `check_balance` method should print and return the current balance.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
account = BankAccount('123456789', 'John Doe', 100)
account.deposit(50)  # Output: Deposited $50. New balance is $150.
account.withdraw(30) # Output: Withdrew $30. New balance is $120.
account.check_balance() # Output: Current balance is $120.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number=None, account_holder=None, balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with the given account number, account holder, and balance.
        
        :param account_number: The account number of the bank account.
        :param account_holder: The name of the account holder.
        :param balance: The initial balance of the account.
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.account_holder = account_holder
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is negative.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount
        print(f"Deposited ${amount}. New balance is ${self.balance}.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is negative or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount
        print(f"Withdrew ${amount}. New balance is ${self.balance}.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check and return the current balance of the bank account.
        
        :return: The current balance.
        """
        print(f"Current balance is ${self.balance}.")
        return self.balance

Example 9.6

Solve the two-degrees-of-freedom system shown in Fig. 9.17 by Laplace transform. Obtain the response given initial conditions initial displacements corresponding to two degrees of freedom are 3 and 5 respectively and initial velocities corresponding to two degrees of freedom are 1 and 4 respectively.

Solution

The equations of motion can be written as

Taking inverse Laplace transform we will get X1(t), X2(t). The program in MATHEMATICA is given below and the response curves are shown for both the displacements.

Forced vibration of two-degrees-of-freedom undamped system

It is required to study the steady state response of the system shown in Fig. 9.21 when the mass m1 is excited by the force F1 = F10 cos(ω t) and also to plot the frequency response curve.

Dynamic equations of equilibrium can be written as

The responses A1 and A2 are shown on page 296 respectively in terms of dimensionless parameter ω/ω1. In the dimensionless parameter ω/ω1 ω1 is selected arbitrarily. It can be seen that amplitudes of U1 and U2 become infinity when ω 2 = ω n21 or ω 2 = ω n22 . These are the resonance conditions for the system, one at ωn1 and the other at ωn2. At all other values of ω, the amplitudes of vibration are finite. It can be noted from the figure on previews page that there is a particular value of frequency ω at which the vibration of first mass m to which force F1 is applied is reduced to zero. This concept is made use of in vibration absorbers.

Vibration absorber

A spring mass system k2m2 tuned to the frequency of the exciting force such that ω2 = k2/m2 will act as a vibration absorber and reduce the motion of the main mass m1 to zero (see Fig. 9.22).

Assuming the motion to be harmonic, the equation for amplitude can be shown equal to that given by Eq. 9.54 by substituting for fundamental frequencies given by Eq. 9.53.

Assume m1 = 1; m2 = 0.2; k1 = 1; k2 = 0.2; ω112 = k1/m1 = 1; ω 222 = k2/m2 = 1. Figure 9.23 shows a plot of Eq. 9.54. If ω = ω22 the amplitude A1 = 0 but the absorber mass undergoes an amplitude equal to

since the force acting on m2 is –F0. The absorber system k2m2 exerts a force equal and opposite to the disturbing force. Thus the size of k2m2 depends on the allowable value of U2.

Figures 9.24 and 9.25 show the response of both degrees of freedom by varying the excited frequency. It is seen that when the excited frequency = 1rad/s the displacement corresponding to the first degree of freedom is zero.

9.24 Displacement (first degree of freedom response) for different excited frequencies.

Figure 9.27 shows the displacement response for this two-degrees-of-freedom system.

Program 9.6: MATLAB program for displacement response of two-degrees-of-freedom under-damped system for forced vibration

%FORCED RESPONSE OF TWO DEGREE OF FREEDOM UNDER DAMPED SYSTEM

Related words :<#sep#>Implement a Python program to simulate a simple game of Rock-Paper-Scissors. The program should interact with the user by prompting for their choice, randomly generate a choice for the computer, and then determine the winner based on the standard rules of the game. The program must handle invalid inputs gracefully by informing the user of the mistake.

**Input:**
- A single line containing the user's choice: either "rock", "paper", or "scissors". The input is case-insensitive and may contain leading or trailing spaces.

**Output:**
- Print the user's choice.
- Print the computer's randomly selected choice.
- Print the result of the game: "You win!", "Computer wins!", "It's a tie!", or "Invalid input. Please enter rock, paper, or scissors."

**Sample Input 1:**
```
rock
```

**Sample Output 1:**
```
Your choice: rock
Computer's choice: paper
Computer wins!
```

**Sample Input 2:**
```
PAPER
```

**Sample Output 2:**
```
Your choice: paper
Computer's choice: rock
You win!
```

**Sample Input 3:**
```
scissors
```

**Sample Output 3:**
```
Your choice: scissors
Computer's choice: scissors
It's a tie!
```

**Sample Input 4:**
```
lizard
```

**Sample Output 4:**
```
Your choice: lizard
Computer's choice: rock
Invalid input. Please enter rock, paper, or scissors.
```

```python
import random

def get_computer_choice():
    """
    Randomly selects and returns the computer's choice from 'rock', 'paper', or 'scissors'.
    """
    choices = ['rock', 'paper', 'scissors']
    return random.choice(choices)

def get_user_choice():
    """
    Prompts the user to enter their choice and returns it in lowercase, stripped of leading/trailing spaces.
    """
    user_input = input("Enter your choice (rock, paper, scissors): ").strip().lower()
    return user_input

def determine_winner(user_choice, computer_choice):
    """
    Determines the winner of the Rock-Paper-Scissors game based on the user's and computer's choices.
    Returns the result as a string.
    """
    if user_choice == computer_choice:
        return "It's a tie!"
    elif (user_choice == 'rock' and computer_choice == 'scissors') or \
         (user_choice == 'scissors' and computer_choice == 'paper') or \
         (user_choice == 'paper' and computer_choice == 'rock'):
        return "You win!"
    elif user_choice not in ['rock', 'paper', 'scissors']:
        return "Invalid input. Please enter rock, paper, or scissors."
    else:
        return "Computer wins!"

def play_game():
    """
    Orchestrates the Rock-Paper-Scissors game by getting user and computer choices, determining the winner, and displaying the results.
    """
    print("Welcome to Rock-Paper-Scissors!")
    user_choice = get_user_choice()
    computer_choice = get_computer_choice()
    
    print(f"Your choice: {user_choice}")
    print(f"Computer's choice: {computer_choice}")
    
    result = determine_winner(user_choice, computer_choice)
    print(result)

# Play the game
play_game()
```<#sep#> (1, m2). The cosine of the angle between the bisector and the first tangent is:

(1*1 + 1*m1)/sqrt(1 + 1)sqrt(1 + m1²)

Similarly for the second tangent:

(1*1 + 1*m2)/sqrt(1 + 1)sqrt(1 + m2²)

Since the angles are equal, these two cosines must be equal:

(1 + m1)/sqrt(2(1 + m1²)) = (1 + m2)/sqrt(2(1 + m2²))

Simplifying:

(1 + m1)/sqrt(1 + m1²) = (1 + m2)/sqrt(1 + m2²)

Squaring both sides:

(1 + m1)²/(1 + m1²) = (1 + m2)²/(1 + m2²)

Cross multiplying:

(1 + m1)²(1 + m2²) = (1 + m2)²(1 + m1²)

Expanding both sides:

(1 + 2m1 + m1²)(1 + m2²) = (1 + 2m2 + m2²)(1 + m1²)

Left side:

1*(1 + m2²) + 2m1*(1 + m2²) + m1²*(1 + m2²)

= 1 + m2² + 2m1 + 2m1 m2² + m1² + m1² m2²

Right side:

1*(1 + m1²) + 2m2*(1 + m1²) + m2²*(1 + m1²)

= 1 + m1² + 2m2 + 2m2 m1² + m2² + m1² m2²

Subtract right side from left side:

[1 + m2² + 2m1 + 2m1 m2² + m1² + m1² m2²] - [1 + m1² + 2m2 + 2m2 m1² + m2² + m1² m2²] =

1 -1 + m2² - m2² + 2m1 - 2m2 + 2m1 m2² - 2m2 m1² + m1² - m1² + m1² m2² - m1² m2²

Simplify term by term:

0 + 0 + 2(m1 - m2) + 2m1 m2² - 2m2 m1² + 0 + 0 =

2(m1 - m2) + 2m1 m2² - 2m2 m1²

Factor terms:

2(m1 - m2) + 2m1 m2² - 2m2 m1² = 2(m1 - m2) + 2m1 m2(m2 - m1)

Factor out 2(m1 - m2):

= 2(m1 - m2)[1 - m1 m2]

Therefore, setting this equal to zero (since left side minus right side = 0):

2(m1 - m2)(1 - m1 m2) = 0

Since m1 ≠ m2 (they are distinct roots), then m1 - m2 ≠ 0, so 1 - m1 m2 = 0 ⇒ m1 m2 = 1

Which is the same conclusion as before. Therefore, m1 m2 = 1. But as the slopes m1 and m2 are 2a₁ and 2a₂, their product is 4a₁a₂ = 1 ⇒ a₁a₂ = 1/4. But from the quadratic equation, the product a₁a₂ = s. Therefore, s = 1/4. Thus, the value of s is 1/4. 

But wait, let's confirm this with an example. Suppose s = 1/4, and we choose a point (r, 1/4) in Quadrant II, so r < 0. Then the quadratic equation for a is a² - 2r a + 1/4 = 0. The product of the roots a₁ a₂ = 1/4, which matches. The slopes m1 = 2a₁ and m2 = 2a₂, so m1 m2 = 4a₁ a₂ = 4*(1/4) = 1, as required. Therefore, regardless of r, as long as s = 1/4, the product of the slopes is 1, satisfying the angle bisector condition.

But wait, how does the position (r, s) affect this? Is s always 1/4 regardless of r? The problem says "Compute the value of s." So perhaps s is fixed at 1/4. Let me see.

But let's think: if s were different, say s = 1/2, then the product a₁a₂ would be 1/2, leading to m1 m2 = 4*(1/2) = 2, which would not satisfy the angle bisector condition. Therefore, only when s = 1/4, the product of the slopes is 1, ensuring that the angle bisector condition is satisfied. Therefore, the answer is s = 1/4. 

Therefore, the value of s is 1/4. Therefore, the answer is \boxed{\dfrac{1}{4}}.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{4}}
</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the value of \( s \) such that the angle formed by the tangents to the curve \( y = x^2 \) from the point \((r, s)\) in Quadrant II is bisected by the line through \((r, s)\) with slope 1.

1. **Equation of Tangents:**
   The equation of the tangent to the parabola \( y = x^2 \) at a point \((a, a^2)\) is given by:
   \[
   y = 2a(x - a) + a^2 = 2ax - a^2
   \]
   Since the tangent passes through the point \((r, s)\), substituting \( x = r \) and \( y = s \) into the tangent equation gives:
   \[
   s = 2ar - a^2
   \]
   Rearranging this equation, we get:
   \[
   a^2 - 2ar + s = 0
   \]
   This is a quadratic equation in \( a \) with roots \( a_1 \) and \( a_2 \).

2. **Product of Roots:**
   The product of the roots \( a_1 \) and \( a_2 \) of the quadratic equation \( a^2 - 2ra + s = 0 \) is given by:
   \[
   a_1 a_2 = s
   \]

3. **Slopes of Tangents:**
   The slopes of the tangents at points \( (a_1, a_1^2) \) and \( (a_2, a_2^2) \) are \( 2a_1 \) and \( 2a_2 \), respectively.

4. **Angle Bisector Condition:**
   The angle bisector of the tangents has a slope of 1. For the angle bisector condition to hold, the product of the slopes of the tangents must be 1:
   \[
   (2a_1)(2a_2) = 1
   \]
   Simplifying, we get:
   \[
   4a_1 a_2 = 1 \implies a_1 a_2 = \frac{1}{4}
   \]

5. **Finding \( s \):**
   Since \( a_1 a_2 = s \), we have:
   \[
   s = \frac{1}{4}
   \]

Thus, the value of \( s \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{1}{4}}\).<#sep#> done by replacing index 0, so grid0 becomes ['#'], and the other grids are '.'.

    return ['#']

### Expectation:
Like the real API gateway, I want to create resource path with the character ":". This is important for our use case because we follow https://google.aip.dev/136 where ":" is commonly used.

### Actual:

### Version:
I use it via the latest of localstack, from looking at the source, this should also be an issue on the latest version of moto.

------

Please add support for ":" in the regular expression here: https://github.com/getmoto/moto/blame/a4fe80d274eb711213e7c3251315b27889a973bb/moto/apigateway/models.py#L1737

Thank you!

```python
# Sample Input
portfolio = Portfolio()
portfolio.add_stock(Stock("AAPL", 150.0))
portfolio.add_stock(Stock("GOOGL", 2800.0))
portfolio.remove_stock("AAPL")
total_value = portfolio.get_total_value()

# Sample Output
total_value  # Should output 2800.0
```

```python
class Stock:
    def __init__(self, ticker, price):
        """
        Initialize a Stock object with a ticker symbol and a price.

        :param ticker: A string representing the stock's ticker symbol.
        :param price: A float representing the current price of the stock.
        """
        self.ticker = ticker
        self.price = price


class Portfolio:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initialize an empty portfolio to hold Stock objects.
        """
        self.stocks = {}

    def add_stock(self, stock):
        """
        Add a Stock object to the portfolio.

        :param stock: A Stock object to be added.
        :raises ValueError: If the provided object is not a Stock instance.
        """
        if isinstance(stock, Stock):
            self.stocks[stock.ticker] = stock
        else:
            raise ValueError("Only Stock objects can be added to the portfolio")

    def remove_stock(self, ticker):
        """
        Remove a Stock object from the portfolio by its ticker symbol.

        :param ticker: A string representing the ticker symbol of the stock to be removed.
        :raises KeyError: If the stock with the given ticker symbol does not exist in the portfolio.
        """
        if ticker in self.stocks:
            del self.stocks[ticker]
        else:
            raise KeyError(f"Stock with ticker {ticker} not found in the portfolio")

    def get_total_value(self):
        """
        Calculate and return the total value of all stocks in the portfolio.

        :return: A float representing the total value of the portfolio.
        """
        return sum(stock.price for stock in self.stocks.values())

Let me think about how to express this as a difference. Suppose I consider something like 1/(x^{2^n} -1) - 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1). Let's compute this difference:

x^{2^n}(x^{2^n} -1)/[(x^{2^n} -1)(x^{2^{n+1}} -1)] = x^{2^n}/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1).

1/(x^{2^n} - x^{-2^n}) = x^{2^n}/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1) = 1/(x^{2^n} -1) - 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1).

This is a telescoping series! Because when we expand the sum, each term cancels with the next one. Let's see:

S = [1/(x^{1} -1) - 1/(x^{2} -1)] + [1/(x^{2} -1) - 1/(x^{4} -1)] + [1/(x^{4} -1) - 1/(x^{8} -1)] + ... 

Each negative term cancels with the positive term in the next bracket. So, the sum telescopes to the first term of the first bracket minus the limit of the negative terms as n approaches infinity. Therefore:

S = 1/(x -1) - lim_{n→∞} 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1).

Now, since x > 1, x^{2^{n+1}} grows without bound as n approaches infinity. Therefore, the limit of 1/(x^{2^{n+1}} -1) as n→∞ is 0. Therefore, the sum S equals 1/(x -1) - 0 = 1/(x -1).

def fractal(L):

    if not L: 

        return ['#']

    idx = L[0]

    P = fractal(L[1:]) 

    S = len(P[0]) if P else 0 # since L wasn't empty, S should be non-zero.

    # create the four grids

    dot_row = '.' * S

    grid0 = [ dot_row for _ in range(S) ]

    grid1 = [ dot_row for _ in range(S) ]

    grid2 = [ dot_row for _ in range(S) ]

    grid3 = [ dot_row for _ in range(S) ]

    # replace the one indexed by idx:

    if idx ==0:

        grid0 = P

    elif idx ==1:

        grid1 = P

    elif idx ==2:

        grid2 = P

    else:

        grid3 = P

    # assemble

    new_size = 2*S +1

    rows = []

    # top rows: grid0 left, then grid1

    for r in range(S):

        row = (grid0[r] if isinstance(grid0[0], str) else ..) 

        # but grid0 is replaced with P, which is a list of strings, so 

        row = grid0[r] + '#' + grid1[r] 

        rows.append(row)

    # add the separator row (all #)

    rows.append('#'* new_size )

    # bottom rows: grid2 and grid3.

    for r in range(S):

        row = grid2[r] + '#' + grid3[r]

        rows.append(row)

    return rows

Hello,

When trying to create a version for a non-exiting lambda the exception is different from the one received from AWS:

sample code:


```
import boto3
import moto


@moto.mock_lambda
def run_me():
    lambda_function = boto3.client("lambda", "eu-west-1")
    lambda_function.publish_version(FunctionName="bad_function_name", Description="v2")


run_me()
```



Exception received when using moto:
```
  File "/somepath/lib/python3.8/site-packages/moto/awslambda/models.py", line 1016, in publish_function
    name = function.function_name
AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'function_name'
```

Exception received without moto:
```
  File "/somepath/lib/python3.8/site-packages/botocore/client.py", line 725, in _make_api_call
    raise error_class(parsed_response, operation_name)
botocore.errorfactory.ResourceNotFoundException: An error occurred (ResourceNotFoundException) when calling the PublishVersion operation: Function not found: arn:aws:lambda:eu-west-1:xxxxxxxxxxxx:function:bad_function_name
```

The desired behaviour is receiving the same exception from boto and not one raised by moto.

I was using version 3.0.7

The binary tree consists of nodes, where each node has a value and up to two children: a left child and a right child. Implement a class `BinaryTree` with the following methods:

- `__init__(self, root_value)`: Initializes the binary tree with a root node containing the value `root_value`.
- `insert_left(self, parent_value, child_value)`: Inserts a new node with value `child_value` as the left child of the node with value `parent_value` if the left child does not already exist.
- `insert_right(self, parent_value, child_value)`: Inserts a new node with value `child_value` as the right child of the node with value `parent_value` if the right child does not already exist.
- `preorder_traversal(self)`: Returns a list of node values in preorder traversal order.
- `inorder_traversal(self)`: Returns a list of node values in inorder traversal order.
- `postorder_traversal(self)`: Returns a list of node values in postorder traversal order.

**Sample Input:**
```
tree = BinaryTree(1)
tree.insert_left(1, 2)
tree.insert_right(1, 3)
tree.insert_left(2, 4)
tree.insert_right(2, 5)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
tree.preorder_traversal()  # [1, 2, 4, 5, 3]
tree.inorder_traversal()   # [4, 2, 5, 1, 3]
tree.postorder_traversal() # [4, 5, 2, 3, 1]
```

```python
class Node:
    def __init__(self, value):
        self.value = value
        self.left = None
        self.right = None

class BinaryTree:
    def __init__(self, root_value):
        """Initializes the binary tree with a root node containing the value root_value."""
        self.root = Node(root_value)

    def _find(self, value, node):
        """Helper method to find a node with the given value."""
        if node is None:
            return None
        if node.value == value:
            return node
        left_result = self._find(value, node.left)
        if left_result is not None:
            return left_result
        return self._find(value, node.right)

    def insert_left(self, parent_value, child_value):
        """Inserts a new node with value child_value as the left child of the node with value parent_value if the left child does not already exist."""
        parent_node = self._find(parent_value, self.root)
        if parent_node is not None and parent_node.left is None:
            parent_node.left = Node(child_value)

    def insert_right(self, parent_value, child_value):
        """Inserts a new node with value child_value as the right child of the node with value parent_value if the right child does not already exist."""
        parent_node = self._find(parent_value, self.root)
        if parent_node is not None and parent_node.right is None:
            parent_node.right = Node(child_value)

    def preorder_traversal(self, node=None, result=None):
        """Returns a list of node values in preorder traversal order."""
        if result is None:
            result = []
        if node is None:
            node = self.root
        result.append(node.value)
        if node.left:
            self.preorder_traversal(node.left, result)
        if node.right:
            self.preorder_traversal(node.right, result)
        return result

    def inorder_traversal(self, node=None, result=None):
        """Returns a list of node values in inorder traversal order."""
        if result is None:
            result = []
        if node is None:
            node = self.root
        if node.left:
            self.inorder_traversal(node.left, result)
        result.append(node.value)
        if node.right:
            self.inorder_traversal(node.right, result)
        return result

    def postorder_traversal(self, node=None, result=None):
        """Returns a list of node values in postorder traversal order."""
        if result is None:
            result = []
        if node is None:
            node = self.root
        if node.left:
            self.postorder_traversal(node.left, result)
        if node.right:
            self.postorder_traversal(node.right, result)
        result.append(node.value)
        return result
```<#sep#>2/3 ≈0.6667, plus 4/15≈0.2667 gives ~0.9333, plus 16/255≈0.0627 gives ~0.996, then +0.0039≈0.9999, and so on. So it's approaching 1. But 1/(2 -1) =1, which matches. So the sum for x=2 is indeed 1/(2 -1)=1, and the partial sums approach 1, which checks out.

Therefore, the general formula is 1/(x -1).

**Final Answer**
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{x - 1}}
</think>To compute the sum \(\sum_{n = 0}^\infty \frac{1}{x^{2^n} - x^{-2^n}}\) for \(x > 1\), we start by simplifying the general term.

The term \(\frac{1}{x^{2^n} - x^{-2^n}}\) can be rewritten as:
\[
\frac{1}{x^{2^n} - x^{-2^n}} = \frac{x^{2^n}}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}
\]

Next, we recognize that this term can be expressed as a telescoping series. Specifically, we observe that:
\[
\frac{1}{x^{2^n} - x^{-2^n}} = \frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}
\]

Thus, the sum can be written as:
\[
\sum_{n = 0}^\infty \left( \frac{1}{x^{2^n} - 1} - \frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1} \right)
\]

When expanded, this series telescopes:
\[
\left( \frac{1}{x - 1} - \frac{1}{x^2 - 1} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{x^2 - 1} - \frac{1}{x^4 - 1} \right) + \left( \frac{1}{x^4 - 1} - \frac{1}{x^8 - 1} \right) + \cdots
\]

Most terms cancel out, leaving only the first term of the first bracket and the limit of the negative terms as \(n\) approaches infinity. Since \(x > 1\), \(x^{2^{n+1}}\) grows without bound, making the limit of \(\frac{1}{x^{2^{n+1}} - 1}\) approach 0. Therefore, the sum simplifies to:
\[
\frac{1}{x - 1} - 0 = \frac{1}{x - 1}
\]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{x - 1}}

Pent<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a class `Employee` to manage employee data in a company's HR system. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the employee's name.
  - `employee_id`: An integer representing the employee's unique identifier.
  - `position`: A string representing the employee's position within the company.
  - `salary`: A float representing the employee's annual salary.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, name, employee_id, position, salary)`: Initializes the employee's attributes.
  - `give_raise(self, amount)`: Increases the employee's salary by a specified amount.
  - `promote(self, new_position)`: Updates the employee's position to a new one.
  - `display_info(self)`: Prints the employee's information in the format: "Employee ID: {employee_id}, Name: {name}, Position: {position}, Salary: ${salary:.2f}".

**Input:**
- The input will be provided as a list of tuples, where each tuple contains the name, employee_id, position, and salary of an employee.
- The input will also include a list of operations to perform on the employees. Each operation is a tuple with the first element being the operation type ('raise' or 'promote') and the subsequent elements being the employee_id and the operation-specific details (amount for 'raise', new_position for 'promote').

**Output:**
- For each 'display_info' operation, output the employee's information in the specified format.

**Sample Input:**
```
employees = [
    ("John Doe", 1, "Software Engineer", 80000),
    ("Jane Smith", 2, "Project Manager", 90000)
]
operations = [
    ('raise', 1, 5000),
    ('promote', 2, "Senior Project Manager"),
    ('display_info', 1),
    ('display_info', 2)
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Employee ID: 1, Name: John Doe, Position: Software Engineer, Salary: $85000.00
Employee ID: 2, Name: Jane Smith, Position: Senior Project Manager, Salary: $90000.00
```

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, position, salary):
        """
        Initializes the employee's attributes.
        
        :param name: str - The employee's name.
        :param employee_id: int - The employee's unique identifier.
        :param position: str - The employee's position.
        :param salary: float - The employee's annual salary.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.position = position
        self.salary = salary

    def give_raise(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the employee's salary by a specified amount.
        
        :param amount: float - The amount to increase the salary by.
        """
        self.salary += amount

    def promote(self, new_position):
        """
        Updates the employee's position to a new one.
        
        :param new_position: str - The new position for the employee.
        """
        self.position = new_position

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the employee's information in the specified format.
        """
        print(f"Employee ID: {self.employee_id}, Name: {self.name}, Position: {self.position}, Salary: ${self.salary:.2f}")

# Creating instances of the Employee class
employees = [
    Employee("John Doe", 1, "Software Engineer", 80000),
    Employee("Jane Smith", 2, "Project Manager", 90000)
]

# Mapping employee IDs to Employee objects
employee_dict = {emp.employee_id: emp for emp in employees}

# Operations to perform
operations = [
    ('raise', 1, 5000),
    ('promote', 2, "Senior Project Manager"),
    ('display_info', 1),
    ('display_info', 2)
]

# Processing operations
for operation in operations:
    op_type = operation[0]
    emp_id = operation[1]
    if op_type == 'raise':
        employee_dict[emp_id].give_raise(operation[2])
    elif op_type == 'promote':
        employee_dict[emp_id].promote(operation[2])
    elif op_type == 'display_info':
        employee_dict[emp_id].display_info()
```<#sep#>Simplify

</think>To simplify the expression \(\frac{\sin{10^\circ}+\sin{20^\circ}}{\cos{10^\circ}+\cos{20^\circ}}\), we will use the sum-to-product identities for sine and cosine.

First, recall the sum-to-product identities:
\[
\sin A + \sin B = 2 \sin \left( \frac{A+B}{2} \right) \cos \left( \frac{A-B}{2} \right)
\]
\[
\cos A + \cos B = 2 \cos \left( \frac{A+B}{2} \right) \cos \left( \frac{A-B}{2} \right)
\]

Applying these identities to the numerator and the denominator:

1. **Numerator:**
\[
\sin 10^\circ + \sin 20^\circ = 2 \sin \left( \frac{10^\circ + 20^\circ}{2} \right) \cos \left( \frac{10^\circ - 20^\circ}{2} \right)
\]
\[
= 2 \sin \left( \frac{30^\circ}{2} \right) \cos \left( \frac{-10^\circ}{2} \right)
\]
\[
= 2 \sin 15^\circ \cos (-5^\circ)
\]
Since \(\cos (-5^\circ) = \cos 5^\circ\), the numerator simplifies to:
\[
2 \sin 15^\circ \cos 5^\circ
\]

2. **Denominator:**
\[
\cos 10^\circ + \cos 20^\circ = 2 \cos \left( \frac{10^\circ + 20^\circ}{2} \right) \cos \left( \frac{10^\circ - 20^\circ}{2} \right)
\]
\[
= 2 \cos \left( \frac{30^\circ}{2} \right) \cos \left( \frac{-10^\circ}{2} \right)
\]
\[
= 2 \cos 15^\circ \cos (-5^\circ)
\]
Since \(\cos (-5^\circ) = \cos 5^\circ\), the denominator simplifies to:
\[
2 \cos 15^\circ \cos 5^\circ
\]

Now, we can rewrite the original expression using the simplified numerator and denominator:
\[
\frac{\sin 10^\circ + \sin 20^\circ}{\cos 10^\circ + \cos 20^\circ} = \frac{2 \sin 15^\circ \cos 5^\circ}{2 \cos 15^\circ \cos 5^\circ}
\]

The \(2\) and \(\cos 5^\circ\) terms cancel out:
\[
= \frac{\sin 15^\circ}{\cos 15^\circ} = \tan 15^\circ
\]

Thus, the simplified form of the given expression is:

A while ago I was reading about quines. It made me wonder if it possible to write a macro in C which will print itself, without writing any function such as main. every piece of code should be only in macro. After a few attempts, I couldn't find the solution.
While searching the web for answer, I found the following code:
#define Q(x) #x;x
char *q=Q(main(){printf("#define Q(x) #x;x\nchar *q=Q(");printf(q);printf(")\n");})

Although part of the code isn't in the #define (isn't macro). Is it possible to implement a macro which will print the program ( quine type program )?
EDIT: if it isn't possible (because the macros are 'made' in the preprocessor step), is it possible to implement a quine which is mostly in macros?

moto 3.0.2

 with the update past 3.0, any of our tests using moto failed with "Resource not Found"
 
 The tests originally looked something like this (which worked under 2.3.2:

 (create table creates the table in use and add a single item to, and returns a dynamodb resource)


 ```
 @mock_dynamodb2
class Test_from_dynamo():
    def setup(self):
        self.dynamodb = create_table()

    def teardown(self):
        # self.dynamodb.Table(TEST_TABLE_NAME).delete()
       pass

    def test_mock_data_in_dynamo(self):
        table = self.dynamodb.Table(TEST_TABLE_NAME)
        all_items = table.scan()
        assert len(all_items["Items"]) == 1
```

please note the recreation of the boto3 resource inside the test.

I am aware of the teardown workaround, however the test is failing with Resource Not Found on the table=self.dynamodb.Table(TEST_TABLE_NAME) line.

so resources set up in setup are not sticking around  into the test itself.

however, if we change the test to:

```
def test_mock_data_successfully_setup_for_comparing_premiums(self):
        dynamodb = create_table()
        table = dynamodb.Table(TEST_TABLE_NAME)
        all_items = table.scan()
        assert len(all_items["Items"]) == 1
```

The test now passes.

( X, Y ) & review page to learn more, visit our Earning Credit page note: ratio.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that simulates basic banking operations. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an account number, account holder name, and an optional initial balance (default is 0).
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. Raise a `ValueError` if the deposit amount is non-positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. Raise a `ValueError` if the withdrawal amount is non-positive or if there are insufficient funds.
- **Check Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.
- **Display Account Details**: Return a formatted string containing the account number, account holder name, and current balance.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The `deposit` and `withdraw` methods will be called with integer or float values.
- The `display_account_details` method should return a string formatted as follows:
  ```
  Account Number: <account_number>
  Account Holder: <account_holder>
  Balance: <balance>.<two_decimal_places>
  ```

**Sample Usage**:
- Initialize an account with account number "123456789", account holder "John Doe", and initial balance 1000.
- Deposit 500 into the account.
- Withdraw 200 from the account.
- Check the balance (should return 1300).
- Display account details (should return):
  ```
  Account Number: 123456789
  Account Holder: John Doe
  Balance: 1300.00
  ```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, account_holder, balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account.

        :param account_number: str, the account number
        :param account_holder: str, the name of the account holder
        :param balance: float, the initial balance (default is 0)
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.account_holder = account_holder
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: float, the amount to deposit
        :raises ValueError: if the amount is non-positive
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.

        :param amount: float, the amount to withdraw
        :raises ValueError: if the amount is non-positive or if there are insufficient funds
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for this withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check the current balance of the account.

        :return: float, the current balance
        """
        return self.balance

    def display_account_details(self):
        """
        Display the account details.

        :return: str, formatted account details
        """
        return f"Account Number: {self.account_number}\nAccount Holder: {self.account_holder}\nBalance: {self.balance:.2f}"
```<#sep#>How many roots does the equation $\sum^{2023}_{r=1}\frac{r}{x-r}=0$ have?

<think>
Okay, so I need to figure out how many roots the equation ∑_{r=1}^{2023} (r/(x - r)) = 0 has. Hmm, let's start by understanding what this equation is. It's a sum of fractions where each term is r divided by (x minus r), and we're summing that from r equals 1 to 2023. The question is asking how many solutions exist where this sum equals zero.

First, let's consider the structure of this equation. Each term in the sum, r/(x - r), has a vertical asymptote at x = r. So, between each pair of consecutive integers from 1 to 2023, there's a vertical asymptote. Now, since there are 2023 terms, the function is defined on intervals (-∞, 1), (1, 2), (2, 3), ..., (2022, 2023), (2023, ∞).

Now, the equation is set to zero, so we need to find the number of real roots where the sum of these fractions equals zero. Let's denote the function as f(x) = ∑_{r=1}^{2023} [r/(x - r)]. To find the number of roots, we need to analyze the behavior of f(x) in each interval between the asymptotes and see how many times it crosses the x-axis.

Let me recall that between two vertical asymptotes, say at x = k and x = k+1, the function f(x) will tend to +∞ or -∞ as it approaches each asymptote from either side. The sign of the function in that interval can be determined by evaluating the limits as x approaches the asymptotes from the left and right.

But how does the function behave between each pair of asymptotes? Let's take a specific interval, say (n, n+1) where n is an integer from 1 to 2022. Let's analyze the behavior here.

First, as x approaches n from the right (x → n+), the term n/(x - n) will dominate because the denominator approaches zero, making this term go to +∞. All other terms in the sum will be finite since x is not near their respective denominators. So, the sum will approach +∞.

Similarly, as x approaches (n+1) from the left (x → (n+1)-), the term (n+1)/(x - (n+1)) will dominate and approach -∞ because the denominator is approaching zero from the negative side. So, the sum will approach -∞.

Therefore, in each interval (n, n+1), the function f(x) goes from +∞ to -∞. By the Intermediate Value Theorem, there must be at least one root in each interval (n, n+1) where the function crosses the x-axis. So, for each of the intervals between 1 and 2023, there's at least one root. That gives us 2022 roots between 1 and 2023.

But wait, there's also the intervals (-∞, 1) and (2023, ∞). What about those?

Let's check the interval (-∞, 1). As x approaches 1 from the left (x → 1-), each term r/(x - r) for r from 1 to 2023 will have denominators (x - r) which is negative because x < r for all r ≥ 1. So each term is r divided by a negative number, so all terms are negative. Therefore, as x approaches 1 from the left, the sum will approach -∞. But what happens as x approaches -∞? Let's see: when x is a very large negative number, each term r/(x - r) ≈ r/x (since x dominates the denominator), so the sum is approximately (1/x) * ∑_{r=1}^{2023} r. The sum of r from 1 to 2023 is (2023)(2024)/2, which is a positive number. Therefore, as x approaches -∞, the sum approaches 0 from the negative side (because x is negative). Wait, no: if x is very large negative, then each term r/(x - r) is r divided by a large negative number, so each term is approximately 0 but negative. So the entire sum would approach 0 from below (negative side). Therefore, in the interval (-∞, 1), the function goes from 0 (approaching from below) to -∞ as x approaches 1 from the left. So the function is decreasing from 0 to -∞. Hence, it never crosses the x-axis in this interval. Therefore, no root in (-∞, 1).

Now, let's check the interval (2023, ∞). As x approaches 2023 from the right (x → 2023+), the term 2023/(x - 2023) dominates and approaches +∞. All other terms, since x is greater than their denominators (x - r) for r < 2023, so they are positive as well. But the dominant term is the last one, so the sum approaches +∞. As x approaches ∞, each term r/(x - r) ≈ r/x, so the sum is approximately (1/x) * ∑_{r=1}^{2023} r, which is a positive number divided by a large x, so approaches 0 from the positive side. Therefore, in the interval (2023, ∞), the function goes from +∞ to 0 as x increases. Therefore, it's decreasing from +∞ to 0, so it doesn't cross the x-axis here either. Therefore, no root in (2023, ∞).

So, so far, we have 2022 roots between 1 and 2023. But wait, could there be more than one root in any interval? Let's check.

Suppose in some interval (n, n+1), the function crosses the x-axis more than once. To check this, we can analyze the derivative. If the function is strictly decreasing in that interval, then it can cross the x-axis only once. Let's compute the derivative of f(x):

f'(x) = ∑_{r=1}^{2023} d/dx [r/(x - r)] = ∑_{r=1}^{2023} (-r)/(x - r)^2.

Each term in the derivative is negative because -r/(x - r)^2. Since (x - r)^2 is positive for all x ≠ r, each term is negative. Therefore, the derivative of the function f(x) is the sum of negative terms, so f'(x) < 0 wherever it's defined. Therefore, f(x) is strictly decreasing in each interval between its vertical asymptotes. Therefore, in each interval (n, n+1), the function is strictly decreasing from +∞ to -∞, so it crosses the x-axis exactly once. Therefore, there is exactly one root in each of the 2022 intervals between 1 and 2023.

Therefore, the total number of roots is 2022.

But wait, let me verify this with a smaller case. Let's take n=2, so the equation is 1/(x - 1) + 2/(x - 2) = 0. Let's solve this.

Multiply both sides by (x -1)(x -2):

1*(x - 2) + 2*(x -1) = 0

x - 2 + 2x - 2 = 0

3x -4 =0 → x=4/3.

So in the interval (1,2), there's exactly one root. That's correct.

Another example: n=3, equation: 1/(x-1) + 2/(x-2) + 3/(x-3)=0. Let's see how many roots. Between each pair of asymptotes (1,2), (2,3), (3, ∞). Let's check behavior:

In (1,2): as x→1+, sum approaches +infty (from first term). As x→2-, the second term dominates and approaches -infty. So, by IVT, one root here.

In (2,3): as x→2+, sum approaches +infty (second term) and as x→3-, third term dominates to -infty. So another root here.

In (3, ∞): as x→3+, sum approaches +infty and as x→infty, approaches 0. So no root.

So, total roots: 2. Which is 3-1=2. So yes, the formula holds. Thus for n=2023, it's 2022 roots.

<#sep#>The Dyscalculia Centre provides a range of information on dyscalculia which can be accessed through the headings above.

If you are interested in being tested yourself for dyscalculia, or testing a child there are details in these three articles:

Testing: information for teachers

Testing: information for parents

Testing: information for adults who think they may be dyscalculic.

SUPPORT MATERIALS

Books of activities to support dyscalculic adults and children

The Dyscalculia Centre has also published several books that can be used to help dyscalulic people of all ages overcome their difficulties with maths.  These books are intended to be used by the dyscalculic person working alongside a teacher or a friend who has average ability in maths.  They are also suitable for a parent who wishes to help her or his child.

Ordering details are given at the end

• Dyscalculia activities 1: addition to division: - copiable resource of activities to teach the four basic functions to children

Dyscalculia activities 1: addition to division works from the basis that virtually everyone who has particular difficulties with mathematics has problems because they have failed to grasp one or more of the fundamental principles of maths, and it is this that causes their subsequent problems. The book goes through these fundamentals using a multi-sensory approach alongside conventional notation of maths.

It is not anticipated that most people will need to work through the whole book; rather the volume is constructed so that the teacher can direct the pupil to work on specific topics in order to clarify issues previously misunderstood.

You will also need to provide a series of coloured counters (such as those used with games of Ludo or tiddlywinks) and for some of the later work, a set of cards.

Dyscalculia activities 1: addition to division is supplied as a download so that you can print out whatever sections you need.  Order code: T1654 £14.95

• Dyscalculia activities 2: shapes, fractions, percentages

This volume follows on from “Practical Activities” and uses the same multi-sensory techniques that have proved successful in the earlier book. As before the book comes as a photocopiable volume and requires no additional equipment or materials save for a collection of coloured counters.

One of the huge benefits of this approach is that it not only works with those who have the genetic disorder which gives rise to dyscalculia, it also benefits those children who are failing to grasp mathematical concepts because of interrupted schooling, home problems or even a maths aversion which has developed because of early failure. Experience suggests that a short period of using multi-sensory techniques can be enough to overcome such problems and bring the pupil back to the class average.

Each activity within the book can be completed within a maximum of ten minutes, thus allowing the teacher or parent to stop the work the moment the child shows signs of maths-aversion, frustration or tiredness.

Order code: T1719  £14.95

• Dyscalculia activities 3: Time and timetables - book of activities for pupils and students who have difficulty telling the time, following the sequence of days, weeks, months, seasons, etc

Time is the ultimate sequence, and children who master the concept of time usually overcome all their problems with sequencing and also find many concepts in maths much easier to handle. Indeed it is noticeable that many children who have difficulties with maths also have particular difficulties with the concept of time.

Dyscalculia activities 3: Time and timetables contains exercises and activities that are designed to overcome such problems and ensure that children have a full ability to handle all the concepts of time – from the passing of seasons to the use of the 24 hour clock and the reading of timetables.

The book contains a range of straightforward tests which will reveal where the child’s particular problems lie. This is followed by a series of activities covering all aspects of time. These activities can be undertaken in the classroom and can be photocopied and taken home. Indeed those children who are able to do five minutes homework a day on the subject of time will find that their understanding of time develops extremely quickly.

The book is available in photocopiable format which means that the activities can be shared with other staff in your department.

Order code: T1712 £14.95

Dyscalculia and the everyday physical world

If you have any engagement with dyscalculia – for example because you are dyscalculic yourself, or because you know someone who is (or might be) dyscalculic, it is important to remember that dyscalculia affects everyday life.

Imagine being at a children’s party with your child, and the host hands you a knife and says, “Could you cut that cake into ten pieces for me?” that might seem the easiest thing in the world.  You’d probably cut the cake in half, and then mark out each half section into five roughly equal bits.  No problem.

All so easy – but for the dyscalculic person, you might as well ask them to carry the cake across a newly laid minefield.  For such a person the issue is impossible to resolve, and worse, the dyscalculic person will know from painful experience, it will seem to everyone looking on to be a very simple task.  They won’t understand the dyscalculic person’s problem.

Our world is in fact based on numbers all day every day, and if numbers are meaningless to you, then anything that involves number, measurement, time, fractions, etc etc, also becomes meaningless.

But what makes it worse is that everyone else doesn’t understand how this can be.  The response to the question of “cut it into ten” of, “I don’t know how,” is liable to bring the answer, “Just cut it into ten,” which by and large is not very helpful.

Indeed, it is this lack of understanding of the fact that what seems obvious and everyday to most people can seem completely incomprehensible to a small number of people, that can make life so awful for many dyscalculic people.  It is a disability that is incredibly hard for most people to imagine because a) it is invisible and b) most people take numbers for granted.

This is why sometimes in the reports that the Dyscalculia Centre issues after a diagnostic test for dyscalculia has been undertaken, we offer some thoughts and help in relation to maths and the physical world.

For example, if a person (be it a child, teenager or adult) takes our online test and reveals that she or he doesn’t have an understanding of fractions, we sometimes suggest that this individual works with a person who does not have dyscalculia on the simple task of dividing things up.

One starting activity can be taking a piece of paper that is circular in shape with the instruction that it has to be cut in half.

That might seem simple and obvious to the non-dyscalculic person – you simply fold the circular piece of paper over on itself, and then cut along the fold, and you have two equal sections.  But that is not always at all the obvious way to proceed for the dyscalculic person.

But when that dyscalculic person has seen the solution and done it, the idea becomes a reality – especially if one then asks, “how many pieces of paper did we have at the start?” (the answer is one).  Then “how many do we have now?” (the answer is two).  “How can we check that they are the same size?” (fit one on top of the other).

Even that last moment of placing one piece of paper on top of the other might seem problematic to some, but once the process has been seen through several times, it slowly becomes understood.

Then the numbers are engaged.  Place the two semi-circular pieces of paper on the table next to each other.   “How many pieces of paper do we have?”

The answer of course is two.  The number “2” is written on each.

“But this is only one of those two pieces isn’t it?” asks the instructor, and once that is agreed, the number one can be written above the number 2, and everyone says, “one out of two”.

From there it is but a step to write ½ and introduce the word “half.”

The point about this is once it is introduced physically and has been repeated a couple of times, it is possible to move on to quarters, and then start adding quarters and a half together, introducing ¾ as one progresses, along with the new terminology and the written maths.

This is classic multi-sensory learning.  The physical objects (the paper) are handled, the words are said and the symbols written down.

It is slow, but over time<#sep#>You are given a JSON file named `students.json` that contains information about a group of students. Each student's data includes a unique student ID, name, age, and a list of grades. Your task is to write a Python program that reads this JSON file and calculates the average grade for each student. The program should handle potential errors such as file not found, invalid JSON format, or missing required fields in the data.

**Input:**
- A JSON file `students.json` with the following structure:
  ```json
  [
      {"student_id": "S001", "name": "Alice", "age": 20, "grades": [88, 92, 85]},
      {"student_id": "S002", "name": "Bob", "age": 22, "grades": [78, 85]},
      {"student_id": "S003", "name": "Charlie", "age": 21, "grades": []}
  ]
  ```

**Output:**
- For each student, print their student ID and average grade formatted to two decimal places. If a student has no grades, their average grade should be `0.00`.
  ```
  Student ID: S001, Average Grade: 88.33
  Student ID: S002, Average Grade: 81.50
  Student ID: S003, Average Grade: 0.00
  ```

```python
import json

def calculate_average_grades(json_file_path):
    """
    Reads a JSON file and calculates the average grade for each student.

    :param json_file_path: Path to the JSON file containing student data.
    :return: A dictionary with student ID as keys and average grade as values.
    """
    try:
        with open(json_file_path, 'r') as file:
            data = json.load(file)
        
        if not isinstance(data, list):
            raise ValueError("The JSON data is not structured as a list of students.")
        
        average_grades = {}

        for student in data:
            if 'student_id' not in student or 'grades' not in student:
                raise ValueError("Student data missing 'student_id' or 'grades'.")

            student_id = student['student_id']
            grades = student['grades']

            if not isinstance(grades, list) or not all(isinstance(grade, (int, float)) for grade in grades):
                raise ValueError(f"Grades for student {student_id} should be a list of numbers.")

            average_grade = sum(grades) / len(grades) if grades else 0
            average_grades[student_id] = average_grade

        return average_grades

    except FileNotFoundError:
        print("Error: The specified file does not exist.")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print("Error: The file is not a valid JSON file.")
    except ValueError as ve:
        print(f"Error in data: {ve}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    json_file_path = 'students.json'
    avg_grades = calculate_average_grades(json_file_path)
    if avg_grades is not None:
        for student_id, avg_grade in avg_grades.items():
            print(f"Student ID: {student_id}, Average Grade: {avg_grade:.2f}")
```<#sep#> -infty. So, by IVT, one root here.

In (2,3): as x→2+, sum approaches +infty (second term) and as x→3-, third term dominates to -infty. So another root here.

In (3, ∞): as x→3+, sum approaches +infty and as x→infty, approaches 0. So no root.

So, total roots: 2. Which is 3-1=2. So yes, the formula holds. Thus for n=2023, it's 2022 roots.

Therefore, the answer is 2022.

**Final Answer**
The number of roots is \boxed{2022}.
</think>To determine the number of roots of the equation \(\sum_{r=1}^{2023} \frac{r}{x - r} = 0\), we analyze the behavior of the function \(f(x) = \sum_{r=1}^{2023} \frac{r}{x - r}\).

1. **Behavior between vertical asymptotes**:
   - The function \(f(x)\) has vertical asymptotes at \(x = 1, 2, \ldots, 2023\).
   - For each interval \((n, n+1)\) where \(n\) is an integer from 1 to 2022:
     - As \(x\) approaches \(n\) from the right, the term \(\frac{n}{x - n}\) dominates and tends to \(+\infty\).
     - As \(x\) approaches \(n+1\) from the left, the term \(\frac{n+1}{x - (n+1)}\) dominates and tends to \(-\infty\).
     - By the Intermediate Value Theorem, since \(f(x)\) is continuous and strictly decreasing (as the derivative \(f'(x) = \sum_{r=1}^{2023} \frac{-r}{(x - r)^2}\) is negative everywhere), there is exactly one root in each interval \((n, n+1)\).

2. **Behavior outside the interval \([1, 2023]\)**:
   - For \(x \in (-\infty, 1)\):
     - As \(x\) approaches 1 from the left, each term \(\frac{r}{x - r}\) is negative, and the function tends to \(-\infty\).
     - As \(x \to -\infty\), each term \(\frac{r}{x - r}\) approaches 0 from the negative side, and the function tends to 0 from the negative side.
     - Thus, there are no roots in this interval.
   - For \(x \in (2023, \infty)\):
     - As \(x\) approaches 2023 from the right, the term \(\frac{2023}{x - 2023}\) dominates and tends to \(+\infty\).
     - As \(x \to \infty\), each term \(\frac{r}{x - r}\) approaches 0 from the positive side, and the function tends to 0 from the positive side.
     - Thus, there are no roots in this interval.

Since there are 2022 intervals between 1 and 2023, each containing exactly one root, the total number of roots is \(\boxed{2022}\).<#sep#> macro, so the macro's expansion must include both the macro's definition and the main function that invokes it.

<#sep#> be written above the number 2, and everyone says, “one out of two”.

From there it is but a step to write ½ and introduce the word “half.”

The point about this is once it is introduced physically and has been repeated a couple of times, it is possible to move on to quarters, and then start adding quarters and a half together, introducing ¾ as one progresses, along with the new terminology and the written maths.

This is classic multi-sensory learning.  The physical objects (the paper) are handled, the words are said and the symbols written down.

It is slow, but over time, and with many dyscalculics, for the first time, fractions have a real life meaning.   Suddenly, instead of the sum

½ + ¼ =

being given the answer 1/6 (which is the most common answer for a dyscalculic person to give), the realisation strikes that the answer indeed ¾ - and that has a real physical meaning.
