Testing for dyscalculia: why and how

This article concerns how adults may be tested for dyscalculia, the reasons that students and adults might want to be tested, and issues relating to admission to sixth form, university and employment for people with dyscalculia.

Please note there are two other articles on testing on this site

Testing for Dyscalculia notes for teachers

Testing for Dyscalculia notes for parents

1: Why should an adult or a student taking GCSE exams and above be tested for dyscalculia?

First, the negative: testing does not make the dyscalculia go away.  It simply confirms (if that is the result) that the individual is dyscalculic, and normally where the problems are.  Nor does it automatically exclude the need to have a pass in GCSE maths or any other exam.  More on this below.

A diagnostic test for dyscalculia may be helpful for several reasons:

• For the school – it enables the special needs department to see exactly what the problem is, and how the teaching of maths may be varied in order to help the individual pupil or student.  In general terms this is the same as dyslexia – once one know the individual is dyslexic or dyscalculic, the teaching can be adjusted to account for this.
• For sixth form, college or university applications.  Some institutions require a GCSE pass at a particular level before students can enter the establishment or take a certain course.  Where the requirement is for laid down because the course being applied for contains maths at a certain level, then it is hard to argue with such a requirement.  However, if (for example) a student wants to study for a degree in English, but the university requires GCSE maths at a certain level as an indication of a general all-round educational ability, it is almost certainly not reasonable to exclude a dyscalculic person, given the provisions of the Equalities Act.  There are more details concerning this Act (which applies to the whole UK) on our website.  The best approach is to make representation to the college or university before doing anything else, to see what their position is on this.  In our experience many individual departments in higher education will waive the requirement for maths at a certain level, providing the course does not require the ability to undertake maths.

2: Compulsory retakes of maths at school.

Government regulations in England state that achieving a level 2 qualification, and in particular a GCSE grade 9 to 4 or A* to C, in both maths and English helps students to progress to further study, training and skilled employment. The maths and English condition of funding requires students to retake the exams until they achieve a pass at the required level.

It is therefore essential that the student is given the right sort of tuition in accordance with their special needs to be able to reach this attainment.  Some schools, we know unofficially ignore the requirement but that of course is a matter for the school.

But all schools in the UK are also bound by the Equalities Act which clearly states that

a) under the Equalities Act the education must be given in a way that recognises the special needs of the student and b) under the compulsory retake regulations the student has to resit the maths exam if it is not passed.

Not to provide teaching that takes account of diagnosed dyscalculia would be a breach of the Equalities Act.  Not to forward a suspected dyscalculic student or pupil for testing would also be a breach of the Act.

Thus quite clearly (in our view) if a student fails GCSE maths and thus becomes part of the compulsory re-sit situation, and there is any suspicion that the student might be dyscalculic, they logically need to be tested and then if diagnosed, given tuition that is commensurate with their disability.

What are you wanting to achieve?

People get tested either because they wish to satisfy their curiosity as to whether dyscalculia is why they can't do maths, or because (by way of example) a university or employer might require GCSE maths at a certain level before entry or appointment, or because they are finding the regular re-taking of GCSE maths exams is emotionally very disturbing.

Because of the laws outlined above, it is important to be very clear why you want to be tested, as this will indicate what route needs to be taken.

The most common reasons for wanting to be tested are:

• Simply to know if I am dyscalculic.   In this regard our on line test is probably the best way forward.  It is not as definitive as seeing a psychologist, but it is only about one sixth of the price.
• To start the process of helping the individual learn maths.   While a psychologist specialising in the subject may well be able to indicate where the problems are, he/she normally don’t indicate how to overcome these difficulties.  Again, in these circumstances the on-line test might be best as if dyscalculia is found, we also supply some teaching materials that can help the individual start to progress in maths.
• To gain admission to a college or university.  Before doing anything else, write to the institution, set out the details of the case, and then ask them if they can waive their admission requirements in the light of this special need.  They may provide their own test for dyscalculia and remedial materials, so this should be done before arranging for any other diagnostic test to be taken.
• To avoid having to take the GCSE maths papers over and over again because of failure to reach the government’s required grade.  However there is nothing in the regulations that recognises dyscalculia.  Therefore the logical solution has to be that the school or college tests the students who have to re-sit the maths GCSE exam for possible dyscalculia, and then if dyscalculia is found, to teach these students in an appropriate way.  If this is not done, it is difficult to see how the school is obeying the requirements of the Equalities Act although we know of no case law which resolves this situation.

But it is important to distinguish why maths is required, beyond the government’s regulation about resits.  In many situations a maths qualification such as GCSE is a pre-requisite for continuing in a course, no matter what, because maths is of the essence in the profession one wishes to follow (for example for an accountant) or the course being studied (for example a maths GCSE may be required before entry to a Chemistry A Level course).

But where a university or employer asks for maths as an entry requirement in order to show a "rounded education" then showing one has dyscalculia can be used to set this aside, under the rules of the Equalities Act.  (See http://dyscalculia.me.uk/special-needs-and-the-law.html for more on the legal situation).

You can find the details of the nearest educational psychologist who specialises in dyscalculia from either the British Psychological Society or the Association of Educational Psychologists - both of whom have web sites containing details of their members.  We believe the cost is around £300 for a session including the writing of the report, but obviously you will need to check.

Our on-line diagnostic test is a lot less expensive because it is online, and thus because it cannot be as accurate as the diagnosis of a psychologist who meets and individual one to one, but many people do find it helpful.  The details are at http://dyscalculia.me.uk/dyscalculia-in-adults.html (see part 4 of the article on that page).

However in conclusion I would stress that although the tests can tell you if it is likely that you have dyscalculia or not they will not of themselves improve your maths.   Although with our test, if we do find dyscalculia is likely to be the cause of your problems<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple restaurant menu management system using a Python class. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Add a Dish**: Add a new dish to the menu with its name, description, and price.
2. **Remove a Dish**: Remove a dish from the menu by its name.
3. **List All Dishes**: Return a list of all dishes currently on the menu.
4. **Search for a Dish**: Search for a dish by its name and return its details if found.

Each dish is uniquely identified by its name. Implement the `RestaurantMenu` class with the methods described above. The implementation should be efficient and easy to understand.

**Input/Output Specification:**

- **add_dish(name, description, price)**: Adds a dish to the menu. No output.
- **remove_dish(name)**: Removes a dish from the menu. No output.
- **list_dishes()**: Returns a list of all dishes. Each dish is represented as a dictionary with keys 'name', 'description', and 'price'.
- **search_dish(name)**: Searches for a dish by name. Returns the dish dictionary if found, otherwise returns `None`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
menu = RestaurantMenu()
menu.add_dish("Spaghetti Bolognese", "Classic Italian dish with meat sauce", 12.99)
menu.add_dish("Caesar Salad", "Fresh romaine lettuce with parmesan and croutons", 8.99)
print(menu.list_dishes())  # Output: [{'name': 'Spaghetti Bolognese', 'description': 'Classic Italian dish with meat sauce', 'price': 12.99}, {'name': 'Caesar Salad', 'description': 'Fresh romaine lettuce with parmesan and croutons', 'price': 8.99}]
menu.remove_dish("Caesar Salad")
print(menu.search_dish("Caesar Salad"))  # Output: None
```

```python
class RestaurantMenu:
    def __init__(self):
        self.menu = []

    def add_dish(self, name, description, price):
        """
        Adds a new dish to the menu.

        :param name: Name of the dish (string)
        :param description: Description of the dish (string)
        :param price: Price of the dish (float)
        """
        dish = {
            'name': name,
            'description': description,
            'price': price
        }
        self.menu.append(dish)

    def remove_dish(self, name):
        """
        Removes a dish from the menu by name.

        :param name: Name of the dish to remove (string)
        """
        self.menu = [dish for dish in self.menu if dish['name'] != name]

    def list_dishes(self):
        """
        Returns a list of all dishes in the menu.

        :return: List of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a dish
        """
        return self.menu

    def search_dish(self, name):
        """
        Searches for a dish by name and returns the dish information if found.

        :param name: Name of the dish to search for (string)
        :return: Dictionary of the dish if found, None if not found
        """
        for dish in self.menu:
            if dish['name'] == name:
                return dish
        return None

\[ \frac{1}{a+k} + \frac{1}{b^2+k} + \frac{1}{c^2+k}. \]

<think>
Okay, let's see. I need to find the maximum value of the expression (1/(a + k)) + (1/(b² + k)) + (1/(c² + k)) where a, b, c are positive real numbers with abc = 1, and k is a natural number greater than or equal to 2. Hmm, okay. So, the variables are linked by the product abc = 1, and I need to maximize this sum. Let me think about how to approach this.

First, maybe I can use some inequality techniques here, like the AM-GM inequality. Since the variables are positive and their product is fixed, maybe symmetry or substitution can help. But the expression isn't symmetric because the terms involve a, b², c². So the variables are treated differently. That complicates things. Let me see.

Since abc = 1, maybe substituting variables in terms of each other could help. For example, if I set a = x, b = y, c = 1/(xy). Then the expression becomes 1/(x + k) + 1/(y² + k) + 1/((1/(x² y²)) + k). Hmm, but that seems messy. Maybe that's not the best approach.

Alternatively, maybe we can use Lagrange multipliers. Since we need to maximize a function subject to a constraint. Let me try that. The function to maximize is f(a, b, c) = 1/(a + k) + 1/(b² + k) + 1/(c² + k), subject to the constraint g(a, b, c) = abc - 1 = 0.

So, setting up the Lagrangian: L = 1/(a + k) + 1/(b² + k) + 1/(c² + k) - λ(abc - 1).

Then, taking partial derivatives:

∂L/∂a = -1/(a + k)² - λ bc = 0

∂L/∂b = -2b/(b² + k)² - λ ac = 0

∂L/∂c = -2c/(c² + k)² - λ ab = 0

And the constraint abc = 1.

So, we have three equations from the partial derivatives:

1) -1/(a + k)^2 - λ bc = 0

2) -2b/(b² + k)^2 - λ ac = 0

3) -2c/(c² + k)^2 - λ ab = 0

And the constraint abc = 1.

Hmm, this seems a bit complicated, but maybe we can relate these equations. Let's see. From equations 2 and 3, maybe we can express λ in terms of other variables and set them equal.

From equation 1:

λ = -1/( (a + k)^2 bc )

From equation 2:

λ = -2b/( (b² + k)^2 ac )

Setting them equal:

-1/( (a + k)^2 bc ) = -2b/( (b² + k)^2 ac )

Multiply both sides by -1:

1/( (a + k)^2 bc ) = 2b/( (b² + k)^2 ac )

Multiply both sides by (a + k)^2 (b² + k)^2 ac * bc to eliminate denominators:

(ac)(b² + k)^2 * 1 = 2b * (a + k)^2 bc * bc

Wait, maybe I need to do the cross multiplication more carefully. Let's see:

Left side: 1/( (a + k)^2 bc ) 

Right side: 2b/( (b² + k)^2 ac )

Cross multiplying:

1 * (b² + k)^2 ac = 2b * (a + k)^2 bc

Simplify both sides:

ac(b² + k)^2 = 2b^2 c(a + k)^2

Divide both sides by c (since c ≠ 0):

a(b² + k)^2 = 2b^2(a + k)^2

Similarly, let's divide both sides by a (a ≠ 0):

(b² + k)^2 = 2b^2(a + k)^2 / a

Wait, but a is related to b and c through abc = 1, so maybe I can express a in terms of b and c. Wait, but if abc = 1, then a = 1/(b c). Maybe substituting that here.

So, substituting a = 1/(b c) into the equation:

(b² + k)^2 = 2b^2( (1/(b c) + k )^2 ) / (1/(b c)) )

Wait, that looks complex. Let's compute the right-hand side:

2b^2 * [ (1/(b c) + k)^2 ] / (1/(b c)) 

First, (1/(b c) + k)^2 = (k + 1/(b c))^2

Dividing by 1/(b c) is multiplying by b c. So,

2b^2 * (k + 1/(b c))^2 * b c = 2b^3 c (k + 1/(b c))^2

So the right-hand side is 2b^3 c (k + 1/(b c))^2

So, the equation becomes:

(b² + k)^2 = 2b^3 c (k + 1/(b c))^2

Hmm, this is getting really complicated. Maybe there's a symmetry or substitution I'm missing here. Let's consider if a = b = c. Since the constraint is abc = 1, that would mean a = b = c = 1. Let's check if this is a critical point.

If a = b = c = 1, then the expression becomes 1/(1 + k) + 1/(1 + k) + 1/(1 + k) = 3/(k + 1). But is this the maximum?

Wait, but maybe when variables are unequal, we can get a larger value. For example, if we let a approach 0, then 1/(a + k) approaches 1/k, but then bc would approach infinity (since abc =1), so b and c would have to go to infinity. But then 1/(b² + k) and 1/(c² + k) approach 0. So the total expression would approach 1/k. Which is less than 3/(k +1) for k >=2. Hmm, so that's worse.

Alternatively, if we set a very large, then 1/(a +k) approaches 0, but then bc =1/a, so as a increases, bc decreases. So, if a is large, then bc is small, so maybe b and c are small. Then 1/(b² +k) and 1/(c² +k) might be larger. Wait, if bc is small, then if b is very small and c is very large, but then 1/(c² +k) would approach 0, but 1/(b² +k) would approach 1/k. Hmm, but since bc =1/a, if a is large, then bc is small. If we set b and c such that b approaches 0 and c approaches infinity, then 1/(b² +k) approaches 1/k and 1/(c² +k) approaches 0. Then the total expression would approach 0 + 1/k + 0 = 1/k. Still less than 3/(k +1). Hmm.

Alternatively, maybe making one variable small and the others adjust accordingly. Let me test some specific values.

Suppose k =2. Then the expression is 1/(a +2) + 1/(b² +2) +1/(c² +2). If we set a=1, b=1, c=1, then each term is 1/3, so total is 1. If we set a=2, then bc=1/2. Let's set b=1/sqrt(2), c=1/sqrt(2). The macro builds the source as a string and the program is that string printed by main (which must exist outside the macro). But user wants main in the macro.

Another approach: The program itself is a single macro expansion of a call to the macro which generates the program's source. So, the program is:

#define Q "\

So, here's the scheme:

Text program = 
    macro definition of Q_PROGRAM(),
    code to print Q_PROGRAM() when run.

Thus, when expanded Q_PROGRAM() gives the program's text.

 #include <stdio.h>
 #define Q                            // the macro's definition
 /* Expands to the program text */
 ...

 But to include the macro inside itself, the macro expansion must recreate the macro's source.

This requires that the macro's expansion starts with how it was defined. 

So the macro's expansion would start with "#define Q...\n rest..." so when printed, that outputs the correct C code.

The rest of the program is just the main function that uses Q to print its own expansion.

Thus, the macro Q in the code would be defined such that expanding it produces the entire program's text.

Including the macro's definition inside.

To encode the macro in its body would require escaping the quotes and backslashes properly.

Wait the problem is that the expansion of Q(...) must include the macro Q's definition again within the string, thereby creating a recursive definition.

Alternatively, here's an attempt:

The key is to define Q() such that it returns the entire program as a string, including the definition of Q() and everything.

Wait here's an idea. The following solution was adapted from the standard C quine, but via macros.

Typically a C self-quine program is:

char q[] = "char q[] = %c%s%c;int main() { printf(q,34,q,34); }";
int main() { printf(q,34,q,34); }

This article concerns how adults may be tested for dyscalculia, the reasons that students and adults might want to be tested, and issues relating to admission to sixth form, university and employment for people with dyscalculia.

Please note there are two other articles on testing on this site

Testing for Dyscalculia notes for teachers

Testing for Dyscalculia notes for parents

1: Why should an adult or a student taking GCSE exams and above be tested for dyscalculia?

First, the negative: testing does not make the dyscalculia go away.  It simply confirms (if that is the result) that the individual is dyscalculic, and normally where the problems are.  Nor does it automatically exclude the need to have a pass in GCSE maths or any other exam.  More on this below.

A diagnostic test for dyscalculia may be helpful for several reasons:

• For the school – it enables the special needs department to see exactly what the problem is, and how the teaching of maths may be varied in order to help the individual pupil or student.  In general terms this is the same as dyslexia – once one know the individual is dyslexic or dyscalculic, the teaching can be adjusted to account for this.
• For sixth form, college or university applications.  Some institutions require a GCSE pass at a particular level before students can enter the establishment or take a certain course.  Where the requirement is for laid down because the course being applied for contains maths at a certain level, then it is hard to argue with such a requirement.  However, if (for example) a student wants to study for a degree in English, but the university requires GCSE maths at a certain level as an indication of a general all-round educational ability, it is almost certainly not reasonable to exclude a dyscalculic person, given the provisions of the Equalities Act.  There are more details concerning this Act (which applies to the whole UK) on our website.  The best approach is to make representation to the college or university before doing anything else, to see what their position is on this.  In our experience many individual departments in higher education will waive the requirement for maths at a certain level, providing the course does not require the ability to undertake maths.

2: Compulsory retakes of maths at school.

Government regulations in England state that achieving a level 2 qualification, and in particular a GCSE grade 9 to 4 or A* to C, in both maths and English helps students to progress to further study, training and skilled employment. The maths and English condition of funding requires students to retake the exams until they achieve a pass at the required level.

It is therefore essential that the student is given the right sort of tuition in accordance with their special needs to be able to reach this attainment.  Some schools, we know unofficially ignore the requirement but that of course is a matter for the school.

But all schools in the UK are also bound by the Equalities Act which clearly states that

a) under the Equalities Act the education must be given in a way that recognises the special needs of the student and b) under the compulsory retake regulations the student has to resit the maths exam if it is not passed.

Not to provide teaching that takes account of diagnosed dyscalculia would be a breach of the Equalities Act.  Not to forward a suspected dyscalculic student or pupil for testing would also be a breach of the Act.

Thus quite clearly (in our view) if a student fails GCSE maths and thus becomes part of the compulsory re-sit situation, and there is any suspicion that the student might be dyscalculic, they logically need to be tested and then if diagnosed, given tuition that is commensurate with their disability.

What are you wanting to achieve?

People get tested either because they wish to satisfy their curiosity as to whether dyscalculia is why they can't do maths, or because (by way of example) a university or employer might require GCSE maths at a certain level before entry or appointment, or because they are finding the regular re-taking of GCSE maths exams is emotionally very disturbing.

Because of the laws outlined above, it is important to be very clear why you want to be tested, as this will indicate what route needs to be taken.

The most common reasons for wanting to be tested are:

• Simply to know if I am dyscalculic.   In this regard our on line test is probably the best way forward.  It is not as definitive as seeing a psychologist, but it is only about one sixth of the price.
• To start the process of helping the individual learn maths.   While a psychologist specialising in the subject may well be able to indicate where the problems are, he/she normally don’t indicate how to overcome these difficulties.  Again, in these circumstances the on-line test might be best as if dyscalculia is found, we also supply some teaching materials that can help the individual start to progress in maths.
• To gain admission to a college or university.  Before doing anything else, write to the institution, set out the details of the case, and then ask them if they can waive their admission requirements in the light of this special need.  They may provide their own test for dyscalculia and remedial materials, so this should be done before arranging for any other diagnostic test to be taken.
• To avoid having to take the GCSE maths papers over and over again because of failure to reach the government’s required grade.  However there is nothing in the regulations that recognises dyscalculia.  Therefore the logical solution has to be that the school or college tests the students who have to re-sit the maths GCSE exam for possible dyscalculia, and then if dyscalculia is found, to teach these students in an appropriate way.  If this is not done, it is difficult to see how the school is obeying the requirements of the Equalities Act although we know of no case law which resolves this situation.

But it is important to distinguish why maths is required, beyond the government’s regulation about resits.  In many situations a maths qualification such as GCSE is a pre-requisite for continuing in a course, no matter what, because maths is of the essence in the profession one wishes to follow (for example for an accountant) or the course being studied (for example a maths GCSE may be required before entry to a Chemistry A Level course).

But where a university or employer asks for maths as an entry requirement in order to show a "rounded education" then showing one has dyscalculia can be used to set this aside, under the rules of the Equalities Act.  (See http://dyscalculia.me.uk/special-needs-and-the-law.html for more on the legal situation).

You can find the details of the nearest educational psychologist who specialises in dyscalculia from either the British Psychological Society or the Association of Educational Psychologists - both of whom have web sites containing details of their members.  We believe the cost is around £300 for a session including the writing of the report, but obviously you will need to check.

Our on-line diagnostic test is a lot less expensive because it is online, and thus because it cannot be as accurate as the diagnosis of a psychologist who meets and individual one to one, but many people do find it helpful.  The details are at http://dyscalculia.me.uk/dyscalculia-in-adults.html (see part 4 of the article on that page).

However in conclusion I would stress that although the tests can tell you if it is likely that you have dyscalculia or not they will not of themselves improve your maths.   Although with our test, if we do find dyscalculia is likely to be the cause of your problems with maths, we do provide, free of charge, some materials you can use with a friend who is not dyscalculic, which can help you improve your maths.

Tony Attwood C.Ed., B.A., M.Phil (Lond), F.Inst.A.M.

Is dyscalculia real, and if so how can those with dyscalculia be helped?

Twenty years ago when my colleagues and I started the Dyscalculia Information Centre we did so because we realised many people were becoming aware of dyscalculia, either because they felt it could<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should encapsulate the following attributes: `title`, `author`, `isbn`, `available_copies`, and `borrow_date`. The `borrow_date` attribute should be initialized to `None` and updated to the current date when a book is borrowed. Implement methods to borrow and return books.

**Input:**
- The `Book` class will be instantiated with the following parameters:
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.
  - `available_copies`: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.

**Output:**
- The `borrow_book` method should return a string indicating the success of the borrowing operation, including the borrow date and the number of copies left.
- The `return_book` method should return a string indicating the success of the return operation, including the number of copies now available.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Instantiate a `Book` object with the title "1984", author "George Orwell", ISBN "1234567890", and 3 available copies.
- Call `borrow_book()`: Output might be "Book '1984' borrowed on 2023-10-05. 2 copies left."
- Call `return_book()`: Output might be "Book '1984' returned. 3 copies now available."

```python
from datetime import date

class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, available_copies):
        """
        Initialize a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        :param available_copies: The number of available copies in the library.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.available_copies = available_copies
        self.borrow_date = None

    def borrow_book(self):
        """
        Borrow a copy of the book if available.

        :return: A message indicating the result of the borrowing operation.
        """
        if self.available_copies > 0:
            self.available_copies -= 1
            self.borrow_date = date.today()
            return f"Book '{self.title}' borrowed on {self.borrow_date}. {self.available_copies} copies left."
        else:
            return "No copies available to borrow."

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Return a copy of the book.

        :return: A message indicating the result of the return operation.
        """
        self.available_copies += 1
        self.borrow_date = None
        return f"Book '{self.title}' returned. {self.available_copies} copies now available."
```<#sep#>² +2). If we set a=1, b=1, c=1, then each term is 1/3, so total is 1. If we set a=2, then bc=1/2. Let's set b=1/sqrt(2), c=1/sqrt(2). Then b² =1/2, c²=1/2.
